It reported about 95% for tabular patent structure/identifier recognition, about 80% for structures and about 75% for identifiers in non-tabular patents, and 92.2% bioactivity extraction accuracy. v2: DECIMER Segmentation + MolNexTR + GLM-4.5V for identifier linking + PaddleOCR + GLM-4.6 for bioactivity and unit normalisation, evaluated on 181 patents across 15 targets with an average above 90% over 3 tasks. It also notes that patent chemical space is largely complementary to ChEMBL. Numbers differ between versions and come from search snippets.
  - checker note: The repo README confirms the current components: DECIMER Segmentation, MolNexTR, GLM-4.5V for identifier recognition, PaddleOCR, and an unnamed LLM that standardises bioactivity. It gives no accuracy figures, no patent counts, and no license, and the arXiv paper was unreachable. The ~95%, 80%, 75%, 92.2% and >90% figures are unconfirmed, as the analyst already flagged.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.10525 ; https://github.com/dahuilangda/BioChemInsight
- [OK] **F12** (number) Reaction diagram parsing shows the clearest evidence on end-to-end versus modular design: VLMs collapse when they must output molecule bounding boxes, but do well when a detector supplies indexed boxes as a visual prompt.
  - details: RxnCaption (arXiv 2511.02384), soft-match F1 on RxnScribe-test. Gemini-2.5-Pro: 24.3 with BROS (model predicts boxes and roles) against 76.1 with BIVP (MolYOLO boxes and indices drawn on the image). GPT-4o: 1.9 against 53.2. Qwen2.5-VL-72B: 12.9 against 68.1. A fine-tuned RxnCaption-VL (Qwen2.5-VL-7B, about 23.4K training images) reaches 86.2 soft F1 on RxnScribe-test and 70.4 on the harder RxnCaption-15k-test, against RxnScribe 80.0/45.9 and RxnIM 76.9/40.5. RxnIM's large synthetic training set barely generalises (a t-SNE plot shows a domain gap). RxnCaption-15k uses a temporal split: papers up to Jun 2024 for training and Jul 2024 for testing, with 400 test images balanced over single-line, multi-line, tree and cyclic layouts.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.02384 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2511.02384 ; hf://papers/2511.02384/paper.md
- [FIXED] **F13** (method) Using a document's own compound identifiers as visual prompts (IdtVP), plus RL with verifiable reaction-level rewards on parsed JSON (Re3-DAPO), beats both box prompts and SFT for VLM reaction parsing.
  - details: arXiv 2603.15011 (Mar 2026, Shanghai AI Lab). IdtVP uses existing identifiers such as bold '1a' as handles. This avoids the distribution shift caused by drawn boxes and reportedly activates pretraining knowledge from interleaved literature, giving state-of-the-art zero-shot soft-match F1 with Gemini 3.0 Pro. Re3-DAPO parses the generated JSON and turns the evaluation metric into dense rewards. The authors argue that SFT forces an arbitrary serialisation order onto set-valued outputs, which causes overfitting, and that RL removes this. They also release ScannedRxn (1950s-1990s scanned diagrams) for out-of-distribution testing. Metrics: soft match (molecules IoU>0.5) and hybrid match (molecules IoU>0.5 and text normalised edit distance <=0.2).
  - CORRECTION: IdtVP and Re3-DAPO (arXiv 2603.15011) use identifiers that already appear in the diagram, plus virtual identifiers drawn in for molecules that lack one. Identifiers are assigned by Mid-Mapper, a Qwen2.5-VL-3B distilled from Gemini 3 Pro, on top of MolYOLO boxes. Zero-shot, IdtVP beats BIVP and BROS: Gemini 3 Pro soft F1 is 88.1 on RxnScribe-test and 78.2 on RxnCaption-15k-test, against 81.3 and 73.4 with BIVP. After Re3-DAPO, the advantage over box prompts largely disappears in-distribution: on RxnScribe-test BIVP+RL reaches 86.9 soft F1 and IdtVP+RL 85.9. IdtVP+RL holds up better out of distribution (ScannedRxn hybrid F1 56.3 vs 45.1, where BIVP+RL collapses into repetition). Zero-shot Gemini 3 Pro + IdtVP still beats the fine-tuned 7B RxnID+RL on soft F1 (78.2 vs 74.5 on RxnCaption-15k-test; 86.5 vs 76.2 on ScannedRxn), though it loses on hybrid F1 (58.3 vs 64.4). ScannedRxn has 200 diagrams. RL ran 870 DAPO steps, about 70 h on 8×H200.
  - checker note: The mechanisms, the metric definitions (IoU>0.5; text NED≤0.2) and the serialization-noise argument (Table 2) are correct. The claim that IdtVP beats box prompts is overstated once RL is applied.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15011 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2603.15011 ; hf://papers/2603.15011/paper.md
- [UNVERIFIED] **F14** (number) ChemEagle (ChemEAGLE) is an MLLM multi-agent system with a planner, observers, R-group-table substitution agents and chemistry tools. It reports 80.8% F1 on multimodal reaction graphics, against 35.6% for the previous state of the art.
  - details: arXiv 2507.20230 (Hanyu Gao group, HKUST). Agents: Planner, Plan/Action Observers, Reaction Template Parsing, Molecular Recognition, Structure-based and Text-based Table R-group Substitution (substructure-match product variants against the template to isolate R fragments, then rebuild reactants), Condition Interpretation, Text Extraction, Data Structure, and a Confidence Scorer (high >=0.85, medium 0.5-0.85, low <0.5). Tools: TesseractOCR, TableParser, MolDetector, Image2Graph/Graph2SMILES/SMILESReconstructor (MolNexTR/MolScribe lineage), RxnImgParser (RxnIM), MolNER, ChemRxnExtractor, and OPSIN/PubChem/CIR name-to-structure. On an OpenChemIE subset, search snippets report about 81 F1 against about 63.7 for OpenChemIE. The current README supports local Qwen3-VL-32B via vLLM. MIT license.
  - checker note: The README confirms the paper ('A Multi-Agent System Enables Versatile Information Extraction from the Chemical Literature', arXiv 2507.20230), the agent and tool list, the confidence thresholds (≥0.85 high, 0.5–0.85 medium, <0.5 low), and the MIT license. It supports local Qwen3-VL-32B (plus Qwen3.5 and 3.8) via vLLM, and its default hosted model is gemini-3.7-flash. The README does not report the 80.8% vs 35.6% F1 or the ~81 vs 63.7 OpenChemIE comparison, and the paper was unreachable.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.20230 ; https://github.com/CYF2000127/ChemEagle
- [OK] **F15** (number) OpenChemIE (MIT), the earlier reference modular toolkit, reaches only 69.5% F1 end-to-end on R-group-annotated reaction schemes and 64.3% accuracy against Reaxys.
  - details: JCIM 2024, 64(14):5521-5534; arXiv 2404.01462. Components: MolDetect, MolCoref (links figure molecules to text labels), MolScribe (OCSR), RxnScribe (reaction diagrams), ChemNER and ChemRxnExtractor (text), with figure/table extraction through an external layout parser. It combines text, figure and table information and resolves R-groups against reaction templates that use '*' attachment points. Pipeline F1 is 69.5% on the authors' R-group-annotated scheme set; reaction extraction accuracy against Reaxys is 64.3%. MolMole later showed that its layout-parser cropping step introduces errors. MIT license.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.01462 ; https://github.com/CrystalEye42/OpenChemIE ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.03777
- [FIXED] **F16** (number) MarkushGrapher-2 (IBM/ETH, CVPR 2026) is the strongest published Markush recognizer, yet reaches only about 54-56% exact CXSMILES accuracy on real patent Markush structures. General VLMs score close to 0.
  - details: From the GitHub README, Markush CXSMILES exact accuracy (%) on M2S / USPTO-M / WildMol-M / IP5-M: MarkushGrapher 2.0 56 / 55 / 48.0 / 53.7; MolParser-Base 39 / 30 / 38.1 / 47.7; MarkushGrapher 1.0 38 / 32 / -/-; MolScribe 21 / 7 / 28.1 / 22.3; GPT-5 3 on M2S; DeepSeek-OCR 0 / 0 / 1.9 / 0.0. IP5-M is a new benchmark of real Markush structures from the 5 major patent offices (reported as about 1,000 structures). Output is CXSMILES plus a substituent table. Architecture: Swin-B vision encoder plus a T5/UDOP vision-text-layout encoder, 831M parameters, with an internal ChemicalOCR module. Training data: 243k USPTO image-SMILES pairs, 91k MolParser, 54k USPTO-MOL-M, and 235k synthetic CXSMILES from MarkushGenerator. Molecule SMILES accuracy: USPTO 89.8, UOB 96.6, JPO 71.0, WildMol 68.4. MIT license.
  - CORRECTION: MarkushGrapher-2 (CVPR 2026, arXiv 2603.28550) scores 56 / 55 / 48.0 / 53.7 CXSMILES exact accuracy on M2S / USPTO-M / WildMol-M / IP5-M. It is no longer the strongest published Markush recognizer. Edison Scientific's MarkushGlyph (2026; Qwen3.5-2B-Base with LoRA; Apache-2.0) reports 58.2 greedy / 60.6 MV@8 on IP5-M, against MG2 re-scored at 53.2 (published 53.7). It also reports 61.2/62.1 on M2S vs 56.0 and 59.5/63.5 on USPTO-Markush vs 55.0. The MarkushGrapher code is MIT, but the HF weights (docling-project/MarkushGrapher-2) are Apache-2.0. The test sets are small: M2S has 103 items, USPTO-Markush 74, and the public IP5-M subset 878 (the paper cites ~1,000).
  - checker note: Every MG2 table value, the 831M-parameter count, the training-data counts, and the molecule SMILES figures (USPTO 89.8 / UOB 96.6 / JPO 71.0 / WildMol 68.4) match the README. GPT-5 scores 3 on M2S, and DeepSeek-OCR 0/0/1.9/0.0.
  - sources: https://github.com/DS4SD/MarkushGrapher ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.28550 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16096 ; https://huggingface.co/datasets/docling-project/MarkushGrapher-2-Datasets ; https://github.com/EdisonScientific/glyph ; https://huggingface.co/EdisonScientific/MarkushGlyph
- [FIXED] **F17** (existence) MarkushGlyph (2026) fine-tunes a 2B VLM (Qwen3.5-2B-Base, LoRA SFT) to emit a structured CXSMILES record directly from a cropped Markush image, with no separate OCR stage.
  - details: arXiv 2607.28532 (found via search; not fully read). A cropped image and a short prompt go to Qwen3.5-2B-Base, which outputs a 'cxsmiles_opt' structured record after LoRA SFT; atom labels, R-group labels, brackets and substituent text are all read by the vision encoder. This shows that small VLMs can be specialised for Markush crops. Benchmark numbers relative to MarkushGrapher-2 were not verified.
  - CORRECTION: MarkushGlyph exists, released by Edison Scientific (GitHub EdisonScientific/glyph; HF EdisonScientific/MarkushGlyph; Apache-2.0). It is a LoRA adapter (rank 128, alpha 128) on Qwen3.5-2B-Base, trained on a 263,158-row index with 5% standard OCSR data mixed in. It maps a cropped patent Markush image to CXSMILES plus an R-group table. CXSMILES is the default output; 'cxsmiles_opt' is an optional format flag that produces <r>LABEL</r> tags. It reports results above MarkushGrapher-2: IP5-M 58.2/60.6 vs 53.2, M2S 61.2/62.1 vs 56.0, USPTO-Markush 59.5/63.5 vs 55.0. A companion model, OCSRGlyph (Swin-B encoder with transformer decoder), scores 93.8 on USPTO. The README does not list an arXiv ID yet, so 2607.28532 is unconfirmed.
  - checker note: The finding's confidence should go from low to high on existence. The benchmark gap over MG2 now has repo-level evidence. Treat it with care: the IP5-M baseline was re-scored by the MarkushGlyph authors, and the benchmarks are small.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/html/2607.28532v1 ; https://github.com/EdisonScientific/glyph ; https://huggingface.co/EdisonScientific/MarkushGlyph ; https://huggingface.co/datasets/EdisonScientific/glyph-datasets
- [FIXED] **F18** (method) Representation choice is a first-order design decision. Plain SMILES cannot encode Markush groups, variable attachment, repeat units, abbreviations as drawn, non-standard bonds or image coordinates, so the field has moved to extended targets: E-SMILES, CXSMILES and CARBON.
  - details: E-SMILES (MolParser, arXiv 2411.11098) is an RDKit SMILES followed by <sep> and an XML-like extension that maps R-groups, abbreviations, dummy atoms and uncertain ring substituents to atom or ring indices, e.g. <a>i:R1</a>. MolParser-7M (7.72M pairs) is public on HF. CARBON (MolRecBench-Wild/MinerU.Chem) is an atom-centric graph JSON with superatoms, dative or bold bonds, oxidation states, radicals, repeat units and 2D coordinates; on MolRecBench-Wild only 2,392 of 5,024 images have valid SMILES. CXSMILES plus a substituent table is used by MarkushGrapher. MolScribe notes that positional variation (substituents floating on rings) and frequency variation cannot be represented in SMILES or MOLfile. GTR-CoT's principle 'faithfully recognize what you've seen' (keep abbreviations as superatoms) gave about +14 points on abbreviated molecules.
  - CORRECTION: The representation descriptions are correct. E-SMILES is SMILES followed by <sep> and an XML-like extension such as <a>i:R1</a>. CARBON supports superatoms, non-standard bonds, oxidation states, radicals, repeat units and coordinates, and only 2,392 of 5,024 revised MolRecBench-Wild images have valid SMILES. MarkushGrapher uses CXSMILES plus a substituent table. MolScribe cannot represent positional or frequency variation in SMILES or MOLfile. Two corrections. (1) GTR-CoT reports ~+14 points over the second-best baseline on abbreviation images for the full GTR-Mol-VLM system, not as an ablation of the 'faithfully recognize what you've seen' principle alone. (2) The MolParser-7M dataset now lives at UniParser/MolParser-7M (AI4Industry redirects there) and is licensed CC BY-NC-SA-4.0, which rules out commercial use.
  - checker note: The point that representation choice comes first stands.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.11098 ; https://huggingface.co/datasets/AI4Industry/MolParser-7M ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05832 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07553 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2205.14311 ; https://huggingface.co/datasets/UniParser/MolParser-7M
- [OK] **F19** (method) VLM-based OCSR improves substantially with structured visual chain-of-thought (graph traversal) and molecule-level reward training (minimum-risk training), not only with more SFT.
  - details: GTR-Mol-VLM (arXiv 2506.07553) interleaves atom and bond predictions as a 'Graph Traversal as Visual CoT' and is trained on GTR-CoT-1.3M (partly real patent images with corrected labels). It beats the second-best method by about 14 points on images with functional-group abbreviations; its descendant GTR-VL-1.4.13 powers MinerU.Chem. COMO (arXiv 2604.23546) applies Minimum Risk Training: it samples candidate SMILES and scores them with a reward combining validity, exact match and Tanimoto, edit-distance or visual similarity. The authors show that the correct molecule often already lies in the MLE model's sample set (e.g., among N=32 samples), so reward-based reweighting fixes exposure bias. They report gains on 10 benchmarks with less training data, and MRT works across architectures.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07553 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.23546
- [FIXED] **F20** (number) RxnBench shows that frontier MLLMs can read text inside reaction schemes almost perfectly, but are weak at structure recognition and score below 50% on full-document reasoning.
  - details: arXiv 2512.23565 (DP Technology). SF-QA: 1,525 questions from 305 schemes; FD-QA: 108 full articles. Of 41 MLLMs, the best SF-QA mean is Gemini-3-Flash-preview at 96.23%, then Seed1.8-Think 93.64%, Gemini-3-Pro-preview 93.61%, Qwen3-VL-235B-A22B-Think 91.77% and -Instruct 85.84%, with GPT-4o at 74.49%. Fact Extraction is about 96% for the top models. The Structure Recognition subtask (image to SMILES/E-SMILES) drops: Gemini-3-Pro 74.63%, GPT-4o 52.24%, Qwen3-VL-235B-Think 84.33%, Gemini-3-Flash 90.30%. No model reaches 50% on FD-QA, which requires cross-page text-scheme-table synthesis and reference resolution ('compound 3a' to structure).
  - CORRECTION: RxnBench (arXiv 2512.23565) contains SF-QA (1,525 questions from 305 schemes) and FD-QA (540 questions from 108 articles). 41 MLLMs were tested on SF-QA and 23 on FD-QA. All SF-QA items are 4-option multiple choice with expert-crafted hard negatives. That includes 'Structure Recognition' (134 questions), so the 74.63% (Gemini-3-Pro), 52.24% (GPT-4o), 84.33% (Qwen3-VL-235B-Think) and 90.30% (Gemini-3-Flash) figures measure selection among SMILES/E-SMILES options, not free-form image-to-SMILES generation. The SF-QA mean scores are correct: 96.23, 93.64, 93.61, 91.77, 85.84 and 74.49. On FD-QA no model reaches 50%; the Gemini family leads at ~46%. Results are reported as Context Reasoning vs Structure Reasoning, for example Gemini-2.5-Pro at 56.82 vs 36.52. 'Reference resolution' is one design dimension and has no score of its own.
  - checker note: The multiple-choice format makes structure-recognition accuracy look better than real OCSR.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.23565 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2512.23565 ; hf://papers/2512.23565/paper.md
- [OK] **F21** (existence) ChemX (NeurIPS 2025 Datasets & Benchmarks) provides 10 expert-curated extraction datasets, including two small-molecule SAR sets with page, section and origin provenance. Agentic systems and GPT-5 still struggle with tables, schematics and SMILES.
  - details: HF datasets ai-chem/*: Oxazolidinones (2,923 rows) and Benzimidazoles (1,721 rows). Fields include smiles, compound_id, target_type, target_relation (qualifier), target_value, target_units, bacteria/bacteria_unified, page_* / origin_* / section_* provenance for target, scaffold and residue (R-group) cells, and the DOI. The other 8 datasets are Complexes, Co-crystals, Nanomag, Synergy, Nanozymes, EyeDrops, SelTox and Cytotox. The paper (arXiv 2510.00795) compares ChatGPT Agent, chemistry extraction agents, GPT-5 / GPT-5 Thinking, and the authors' single agent with controlled document preprocessing. Snippets suggest GPT-5 SMILES F1 near 0.00 on the Complexes baseline, which was not verified. The authors cite domain terminology, complex tables/schematics and context ambiguity as persistent failure modes. MIT license.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.00795 ; https://github.com/ai-chem/ChemX ; https://huggingface.co/datasets/ai-chem/Oxazolidinones ; https://huggingface.co/datasets/ai-chem/Benzimidazoles
- [FIXED] **F22** (lesson) Compound-label coreference (linking 'compound 12a' or 'Example 212' to a drawn structure) is now handled as an explicit module in every serious system, and it is a dominant error source at document level.
  - details: Implementations: OpenChemIE MolCoref; Uni-Parser-LD group-based layout that jointly detects molecule-identifier pairs and Markush descriptions and reorders by group; MinerU.Chem Mid-Mapper (from RxnID), which prioritises frequent, reliable numeric labels; Doc2SAR's fine-tuned Qwen2.5-VL that resolves IDs from surrounding text; BioMiner's 'full structure coreference' task (962 images, 5,105 pairs). BioMiner's end-to-end numbers isolate the cost: values are recalled at about 0.85 and structures at about 0.58, but joint structure+bioactivity recall falls to about 0.33 (F9). RxnBench FD-QA includes a 'reference resolution' category on which no model reaches 50% overall (F20).
  - CORRECTION: Molecule-identifier linking is an explicit module in many leading systems: OpenChemIE MolCoref, Uni-Parser-LD grouping, MinerU.Chem Mid-Mapper (from RxnID, a Qwen2.5-VL-3B distilled from Gemini 3 Pro), Doc2SAR (unverified), BioMiner's coreference task, and ChemEagle. It is not in every system. MolMole and MolParser-Mobile have no identifier-linking module. The BioMiner costs are correct: value recall 0.850, structure recall 0.580, joint structure+bioactivity recall 0.328. RxnBench does not report a separate reference-resolution score; it reports FD-QA overall (<50% for every model) and Context vs Structure Reasoning.
  - checker note: The core lesson, that coreference and joining are the main error source at document level, stands.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.15098 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03525 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21625 ; https://github.com/jiaxianyan/BioMiner ; https://github.com/CrystalEye42/OpenChemIE ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15011 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.03777
- [OK] **F23** (lesson) R-group and SAR-table enumeration into full structures is solved only by rule- or tool-based substitution agents. Published accuracies cluster around 0.70 F1, and a major 2026 system (MinerU.Chem) explicitly does not support it.
  - details: OpenChemIE: 69.5% F1 on its R-group scheme dataset. BioMiner: Markush-enumeration task on 355 images with 3,513 scaffold-R-group-coreference pairs; one README fetch reported F1 0.70 and another listed no number, so treat this as uncertain. ChemEagle: Structure-based and Text-based R-group Substitution agents use RDKit substructure matching and TableParser, then attach substituents to the template. MinerU.Chem section 7 says automated expansion of Markush/R-group substitution patterns is not supported yet, because it needs joint understanding of scaffold, substituent definitions, table fields and instantiation. RxnBench includes Markush enumeration questions. Takeaway: enumeration is best done deterministically (RDKit, with R-labels mapped to attachment atoms) after VLM reading, not generated as free text.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.01462 ; https://github.com/jiaxianyan/BioMiner ; https://github.com/CYF2000127/ChemEagle ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03525 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.23565
- [OK] **F24** (existence) USPTO bulk data contains chemical structures as embedded MOL and ChemDraw CDX files (complex work units) alongside TIFF images. This is a large free source of paired image-structure supervision from real patents, and MolScribe used 680K such pairs.
  - details: The 'Patent Grant Full Text Data with Embedded TIFF Images' product (Red Book, WIPO ST.36, weekly since 2001) includes complex work units: tables, math, chemical structures (MOL/CDX) and sequences. In application XML (ICE DTDs), chemical structures are external CDX and MOL attachments; the SGML/XML text-only grant products reference CWU files but omit them. MolScribe (arXiv 2205.14311) trained on 680K USPTO image-MOL pairs plus 1M PubChem Indigo renders. It notes that the data are noisy and that exact atom coordinates must be approximated from MOL coordinates. The USPTO, CLEF and Staker OCSR benchmarks come from the same source, so de-duplication by patent is needed. MOL files in CWUs often contain generic or R-group structures.
  - sources: https://data.commerce.gov/node/141470 ; https://data.amerigeoss.org/dataset/patent-grant-full-text-1976-present ; https://data.amerigeoss.org/dataset/patent-application-publication-full-text-2001-present ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2205.14311
- [OK] **F25** (number) PatCID (IBM, Nat Commun 2024) is an open patent structure database built with DECIMER segmentation and MolGrapher. On a random-set recall test it retrieves more molecules than Reaxys, SciFinder, Google Patents and SureChEMBL.
  - details: About 81M structure images and 14M unique structures (Zenodo: 13M molecules, 80M images, 1.2M documents) from USPTO, EPO, JPO, KIPO and CNIPA. Molecule recall on a random document set: PatCID 56.0%, Reaxys 53.5%, SciFinder 49.5%, Google Patents 41.5%, SureChEMBL 23.5%. The abstract reports recall only, not precision. The pipeline is open source (MIT). This is the clearest published head-to-head of open and commercial patent structure coverage, and it shows that even commercial manually curated databases miss about half of the depicted molecules.
  - sources: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC11297020/ ; https://zenodo.org/record/10572869 ; https://github.com/DS4SD/PatCID ; https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-024-50779-y
- [UNVERIFIED] **F26** (number) BindingDB is the main human-curated source of patent bioactivity data. Its semi-automated, double-curator workflow has produced about 1.29M measurements from 8,457 US patents, which can serve as gold labels for patent extraction.
  - details: BindingDB in 2024 (NAR 53(D1):D1633-D1644, Jan 2025): 2.9M measurements across 1.3M compounds, with growth 'largely attributable' to curation of US patents. The site reports 8,457 patents and 1,294,922 measurements as of 28 Dec 2025, up from 7,006 patents and 1,091,114 measurements on 27 Mar 2024. Curation workflow (UCSD dataset record): software preloads document data into a curation interface, a curator checks it against the document, and a second curator reviews it. An older NAR paper cites about 35,000 patent measurements per year. Records link the patent number and example number (often inside the ligand name), target, assay description, value and qualifier, which is what is needed to auto-build (patent, record) training and eval pairs.
  - checker note: bindingdb.org, PMC and the UCSD library were all unreachable. The NAR 2025 facts (~2.9M measurements, ~1.3M compounds, growth driven by US patents) agree with the checker's background knowledge. The site counts as of 28 Dec 2025 (8,457 patents; 1,294,922 measurements) and the double-curator workflow could not be confirmed.
  - sources: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC11701568/ ; https://www.bindingdb.org/bind/ ; https://library.ucsd.edu/dc/object/bb1479731z ; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11701568/
- [UNVERIFIED] **F27** (existence) ChEMBL takes patent bioactivity mainly by importing BindingDB and SureChEMBL. ChEMBL 36 tripled the number of BindingDB patent assays, and literature extraction still mixes NLP with manual curation.
  - details: ChEMBL 36 announcement: BindingDB-sourced patent assays rose from 4,117 to 13,847, and chemical-probe data were extracted from literature with a combination of NLP and manual extraction. ChEMBL 35: about 2.5M compounds and 1.7M assays. A 2025 J Cheminform paper reports about 57K compounds, 1,673 targets and about 184K bioactivities derived from SureChEMBL patents. ChEMBL activities carry standard_type, standard_relation (=, <, >, ~), standard_value, standard_units, pChEMBL and data_validity_comment (e.g., 'Potential transcription error', 'Outside typical range'). This defines a ready-made target schema and normalisation reference for extracted records.
  - checker note: The EBI and Springer domains were unreachable. The ChEMBL activity-table fields (standard_type, standard_relation, standard_value, standard_units, pChEMBL, data_validity_comment) are well documented and correct. The ChEMBL 36 BindingDB patent-assay counts (4,117 → 13,847) and the 2025 J Cheminform SureChEMBL figures were not verified.
  - sources: https://www.ebi.ac.uk/about/news/updates-from-data-resources/chembl-36/ ; https://link.springer.com/article/10.1186/s13321-025-00963-z ; https://chembl.gitbook.io/chembl-interface-documentation/frequently-asked-questions/chembl-data-questions
- [UNVERIFIED] **F28** (existence) SureChEMBL 2.0 (EMBL-EBI, 2025) rebuilt its patent annotation pipeline: LeadMine for text since 2025, image-to-structure for US/EP/WO documents, with a new image-to-structure tool not yet announced.
  - details: Per the SureChEMBL documentation, chemistry annotation covers US, EP, WO, CN and JP documents; image-to-structure applies to US, EP and WO; NextMove LeadMine has handled text annotation since 2025; a second image-to-structure tool is to be announced. EMBL-EBI also added AI annotations with Europe PMC, and SureChEMBL had passed 30M PubChem submissions by Jul 2025. In the PatCID comparison (F25), legacy SureChEMBL recalled only 23.5% of depicted molecules. NextMove does not publish precision/recall figures for LeadMine, PatFetch or Pistachio; Pistachio's reactions mostly come from patent experimental text (about 4.2M USPTO and 0.9M EPO).
  - checker note: The SureChEMBL documentation, EMBL news and NextMove pages were unreachable. None of the specific claims could be checked: LeadMine since 2025, image-to-structure for US/EP/WO only, the new tool 'to be announced', or 30M PubChem submissions.
  - sources: https://chembl.gitbook.io/surechembl/patent-annotation/chemistry-annotation ; https://www.ebi.ac.uk/about/news/updates-from-data-resources/surechembl-2-0-is-here/ ; https://www.embl.org/news/science/ai-annotations-increase-patent-data-in-surechembl/ ; https://www.nextmovesoftware.com/pistachio.html
- [UNVERIFIED] **F29** (number) HARVEST (bioRxiv, Mar 2026) is an agentic LLM pipeline that mined USPTO pharmaceutical patents at scale for bioactivity. It reports agreement with human or BindingDB curation of roughly 80-91%, at about $0.11 per document.
  - details: Applied to 164,877 patents. Reported output differs by version and source: 3.15M records with 80% agreement against a BindingDB human-curated US-patent corpus (v2 summary, described as a conservative lower bound), versus 3.36M records with 91% agreement and lower unit-conversion error rates than human curators (v1 summary). It introduces H-Bench, a structurally held-out benchmark on which Boltz-2 degrades for novel scaffolds and uncharacterised targets. Reported as accepted to a NeurIPS 2026 AI4DD workshop. How structures were obtained (USPTO MOL/CDX versus OCSR versus names) was not confirmed. Numbers come from search summaries, not the full text.
  - checker note: bioRxiv was unreachable and no GitHub repo was found for HARVEST. The analyst already reports conflicting v1/v2 numbers (3.36M records at 91% vs 3.15M at 80%). Treat all figures as unconfirmed.
  - sources: https://www.biorxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.03.15.711910v2.full ; https://www.biorxiv.org/content/10.64898/2026.03.15.711910v1
- [OK] **F30** (lesson) Ground-truth bioactivity values are inherently noisy across sources. Evaluation should therefore compare extracted values with the source document, not with a cross-assay consensus, and normalisation errors (units, qualifiers) need explicit checks.
  - details: Landrum & Riniker, JCIM 2024, 64(5):1560-1567. Pairs of ChEMBL IC50 measurements for the same compound and target from different assays, with minimal curation: about 65% differ by more than 0.3 log units, 27% by more than 1 log unit, and Kendall tau = 0.51. With maximal metadata matching: 48% differ by more than 0.3 log, tau = 0.71. Ki data are similarly noisy. CAPRICHO (a ChEMBL processing tool) flags probable unit-annotation errors as differences of exactly 3.0 or 6.0 log units (e.g., nM/µM/mM confusion) and keeps non-molar endpoints (% inhibition, clearance, solubility) separate from pChEMBL. Hard sub-problems include qualifiers (<, >, ~, ranges), unit families, assay conditions in footnotes, and multiple values per compound (n, ± SD).
  - sources: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC10934815/ ; https://capricho.readthedocs.io/en/stable/concepts.html ; https://pubs.acs.org/doi/10.1021/acs.jcim.4c00049 ; https://github.com/David-Araripe/Capricho
- [FIXED] **F31** (lesson) Across chemistry-specific systems, the best published results come from hybrid modular designs: specialist detectors and OCSR models for perception, and an LLM or VLM for reading, linking and reasoning. Pure end-to-end frontier VLMs lag by 18-50+ points on structure-centric subtasks.
  - details: Evidence: OCSR, MinerU.Chem 93.02 vs GPT-5.6-Sol 74.87 SMILES exact match (F4); OCSR, MolParser-Mobile 0.80 vs Qwen3.5-397B 0.35 on BioVista (F7); SAR tables, Doc2SAR 80.78% vs end-to-end GPT-4o 29.30% table recall (F10); reactions, VLM with detector visual prompts vs VLM predicting boxes, 76.1 vs 24.3 soft F1 for Gemini-2.5-Pro (F12); BioMiner's design rationale is to let MLLMs reason about visual relations while chemistry tools construct molecules exactly (F9). Counter-evidence for end-to-end: Logics-Parsing (one 7B VLM) has the best chemistry NED among parsers but is still weak (0.519); MolMole shows that page-level end-to-end detection beats cropping through a generic layout parser (F8); Uni-Parser found end-to-end OCSR (image to E-SMILES) beats graph-based OCSR at scale (F6). The pattern is end-to-end within each module, modular across modules, with grouping and provenance preserved.
  - CORRECTION: Hybrid designs (specialist detector and OCSR plus a VLM or LLM for linking) hold most of the top published results: MinerU.Chem 93.02 vs GPT-5.6-Sol 74.87 SMILES; graph accuracy 79.66 vs 36.41; MolParser-Mobile 0.801 vs Qwen3.5-397B 0.347 on BioVista. The gap with frontier VLMs is closing and sometimes reverses. On MolRecBench-Wild, GPT-5.6-Sol (74.87), Gemini-3.5-flash-thinking (66.85) and Claude-opus-4-8 (65.47) all beat the older specialists MolScribe (62.29) and MolNexTR (62.50). On reaction parsing, zero-shot Gemini 3 Pro with identifier prompts beats a fine-tuned 7B RL model on soft F1 (78.2 vs 74.5 on RxnCaption-15k-test; 86.5 vs 76.2 on ScannedRxn). The RxnCaption 76.1 vs 24.3 result compares two prompting strategies on the same VLM (Gemini with detector-drawn boxes vs predicting boxes), not a specialist against a VLM. The Doc2SAR 80.78 vs 29.30 comparison is unverified.
  - checker note: The design pattern (end-to-end within each module, modular across modules, with provenance kept) is reasonable. The '18–50+ point lag' framing is too broad.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03525 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05807 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21625 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.02384 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.03777 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.15098 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15011
- [FIXED] **F32** (method) The 2025-2026 consensus training recipe for document-chemistry models is data-centric. Synthetic pretraining is followed by real-document pseudo-labels filtered by multi-model consensus, a small human SFT set, hard-sample mining, and RL or preference training on verifiable rewards.
  - details: Examples: MinerU2.5-Pro (fixed architecture, +2.71 points from data: diversity- and difficulty-aware sampling, cross-model consistency verification, judge-and-refine, then GRPO); MolParser-Mobile (unanimous 3-model pseudo-labels, then 91.2k human SFT, on-policy distillation, and DPO on 500 pairs); Uni-Parser (active learning plus ensemble-confidence self-training, 95% less annotation); Logics-Parsing (hard-sample mining for RL: pages whose SFT normalised edit distance falls in [0.5, 0.8], about 8K pages, GRPO for 250 steps); olmOCR 2 (GRPO with binary unit tests generated from synthetic HTML-rendered documents, +14.2 points on olmOCR-Bench over 6 months); Re3-DAPO and COMO (F13, F19). Uni-Parser additionally found synthetic layout data harmful once real data was plentiful.
  - CORRECTION: The data-centric recipe pattern is real, but two details need fixing. Logics-Parsing's ~8K RL set is ~4K manually curated complex-layout pages plus ~4K pages mined from the SFT NED range [0.5, 0.8], trained with GRPO for 250 steps at lr 1e-6. olmOCR 2's +14.2 points on olmOCR-Bench over 6 months comes from RLVR with binary unit tests together with a better base model, model souping, tuned inference and bug fixes, so it is not RL alone. The other examples check out: MinerU2.5-Pro gains +2.71 on a fixed 1.2B model; MolParser-Mobile uses 3-model consensus pseudo-labels, 91.2k SFT samples, on-policy distillation and DPO on 500 pairs; Uni-Parser cut annotation volume by 95% and found synthetic layout data harmful.
  - checker note: A counterexample worth adding: MolSeek-OCR (arXiv 2604.03476) found that RL-style post-training (GSPO, ReFT) did not improve exact SMILES match, and that full-parameter SFT of DeepSeek-OCR-2 failed without a LoRA-first stage.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.04771 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05807 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.15098 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19760 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.19817
- [FIXED] **F33** (number) Detection is no longer the bottleneck for molecules on pages. Modern detectors reach about 0.98-0.99 mAP50, so remaining page-level losses come from OCSR, grouping and linking.
  - details: MolDetv2-Doc-n: mAP50 0.9936 and mAP50:95 0.9544 on MolDet-Bench-Doc (447 pages, 2,178 molecules), 3.1 ms on a T4. Uni-Parser-LD: mAP50:95 0.9679 at 10.1 ms. MolYOLO: P/R 0.98/0.98 on MolDet-33k-test (trained on 219,721 annotated boxes from about 3,000 papers). MolMole ViDetect: AP(.5:.95) 0.914 on patents and 0.928 on articles. Small objects remain hard on BioVista: AP-small 0.050 for MolDetV2 and at most 0.034 for others. Grouping molecules with identifiers and Markush text (Uni-Parser-LD) is the more valuable next step.
  - CORRECTION: On curated in-distribution page benchmarks, molecule detection is nearly saturated: MolDetv2-Doc-n 0.9936 mAP50 and 0.9544 mAP50:95 at 3.1 ms on a T4; Uni-Parser-LD 0.9679 mAP50:95 at 10.1 ms; MolYOLO P/R 0.98/0.98; MolMole ViDetect AP 0.914/0.928. It is not saturated on harder external sets. On BioVista, MolDetV2 gets only AP50 0.922, mAP 0.747 and AP-small 0.050. On scanned historical diagrams, MolYOLO errors alone cost 12.9 F1 points in the IdtVP stage-wise analysis (ScannedRxn). Detection is a minor error source on modern digital PDFs and a real one on small or scanned depictions.
  - checker note: All the individual numbers are correct. The general conclusion that detection is solved needs the qualification above.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05807 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.02384 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.03777 ; https://github.com/jiaxianyan/BioMiner ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15011
- [OK] **F34** (lesson) Stereochemistry and Markush remain the weakest OCSR slices even for specialists. Chirality is repeatedly named as the open problem, and image-to-string models need explicit fixes.
  - details: BioVista OCSR chiral accuracy: MolParser-Mobile 0.653, MolParser 1.0 0.352, MolGlyph 0.504, MolMiner 0.497, MolScribe 0.481, against 0.75-0.80 for full or non-chiral structures. Uni-Parser lists chirality as the main remaining OCSR challenge. MolParser-Mobile future work proposes chirality-aware RL rewards. MolScribe sidesteps the problem by predicting atom coordinates and deriving @/@@ with RDKit rules, arguing that stereo requires geometric reasoning that sequence models lack. MolScribe's metric ignores cis/trans because ground truth often lacks it. On MolRecBench-Wild subset C (chemical-semantic labels), even MinerU.Chem drops from 98.28 to 70.13 SMILES accuracy.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05807 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.15098 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2205.14311 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03525 ; https://github.com/jiaxianyan/BioMiner
- [UNVERIFIED] **F35** (number) Reaction-centric multimodal pipelines report high accuracy within narrow domains: MERMaid 87% end-to-end and its predecessor MERMES at least 96%. Results transfer poorly across layout distributions.
  - details: MERMaid (Aspuru-Guzik group, ChemRxiv Mar 2025, not peer reviewed) has three modules: VisualHeist (segments figures and tables from PDFs), DataRaider (VLM extraction into a reaction schema) and KGWizard (builds a knowledge graph). It reports 87% end-to-end accuracy and is described as topic-agnostic. MERMES (Chem Sci 2024) reported at least 96% on electrosynthesis reaction-diagram parsing and cross-modality dependency resolution. Contrast with RxnCaption-15k-test, where layout-diverse real data drops RxnScribe from 80.0 to 45.9 soft F1 (F12). In-domain accuracy overstates generality.
  - checker note: ChemRxiv and RSC were unreachable, and no MERMaid repo turned up in GitHub search. The 87% (MERMaid) and ≥96% (MERMES) figures and the peer-review status as of Oct 2026 are unconfirmed. The comparison with RxnCaption-15k (RxnScribe dropping from 80.0 to 45.9) is confirmed.
  - sources: https://chemrxiv.org/engage/chemrxiv/article-details/67c6170c6dde43c90858b305 ; https://pubs.rsc.org/en/content/articlelanding/2024/sc/d4sc04630g
- [UNVERIFIED] **F36** (lesson) Classic rule- and NLP-based chemistry toolkits (ChemDataExtractor 2.x) are being displaced by LLM-based extraction, but no tool is yet judged adequate for fully automated large-scale use.
  - details: ChemDataExtractor 2.0 (2021) reported 92.2% precision for nested table-based extraction across 26 journals. A 2025 comparison (arXiv 2512.09370) concludes that CDE 'appears to be gradually being replaced' by LLM tools, yet 'there is no tool that can achieve a level suitable for large-scale automated use'. A 2026 RSC Digital Discovery study benchmarking CDE, ChatExtract, LangChain and Kimi found encouraging precision but extraction completeness below expectations. A vendor (Patsnap) claims 95.5% OCSR precision and 88.4% NER precision; these are unaudited vendor figures.
  - checker note: arXiv, RSC and Patsnap were unreachable, and the paper is not indexed on Hugging Face. None of these were checked: the ChemDataExtractor 2.0 table-extraction precision of 92.2%, the quotes from the 2025 comparison paper, the 2026 Digital Discovery benchmark, or the vendor's 95.5%/88.4% claims (which the analyst already called unaudited).
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.09370 ; https://pubs.rsc.org/en/content/articlehtml/2026/dd/d5dd00482a ; https://www.patsnap.com/resources/blog/articles/best-tools-to-extract-sar-data-from-pharma-patents/
- [FIXED] **F37** (lesson) Benchmark construction practice in 2025-2026 is temporal splits, difficulty-stratified subsets, expert double review, provenance fields and dual-track metrics (SMILES exact match plus graph exact match). Contamination and version drift are real risks.
  - details: RxnCaption and MolDet-33k: train on papers up to Jun 2024, test on Jul 2024. MolRecBench-Wild: MOSAIC-stratified A/B/C subsets, at least 2 QC rounds, 47 annotators; its revision changed the evaluable SMILES subset and moved MolScribe from 41.05 to 62.29. BioVista: 6 component tasks plus end-to-end. ChemX: page, section and origin per field. MinerU2.5-Pro fixed element-matching biases in OmniDocBench v1.5 and added a Hard subset (v1.6). Public OCSR sets (USPTO, CLEF, UOB, JPO, Staker) are saturated (>90%) and overlap USPTO training sources. MolScribe excluded benchmark patents from training.
  - CORRECTION: The practices are real: temporal splits (RxnCaption/MolDet-33k: training on papers up to Jun 2024, July 2024 held out), MOSAIC-stratified A/B/C subsets, at least 2 QC rounds with 47 annotators, version drift (MolScribe 41.05 → 62.29), page/section/origin provenance in ChemX, and OmniDocBench v1.6 adding a Hard subset. Two fixes. (1) Not all public OCSR sets are saturated. USPTO and UOB exceed 90%, but JPO is ~71–81%, CLEF ~85% and WildMol ~68–77% for the top models. (2) BioVista has 6 tasks in total, with triplet extraction and structure-bioactivity annotation as the end-to-end ones, not '6 component tasks plus end-to-end'. Also add that many 2026 benchmarks are very small: M2S has 103 items, USPTO-Markush 74, public IP5-M 878, RxnScribe-test 138 images (whose authors say small score changes lack statistical significance), and MolRecBench-Wild subset C has only 298 SMILES.
  - checker note: Small test sets matter directly for an autoresearch-style loop that keeps or discards changes on a single scalar.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.02384 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05832 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03525 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.04771 ; https://github.com/jiaxianyan/BioMiner ; https://github.com/DS4SD/MarkushGrapher ; https://huggingface.co/datasets/docling-project/MarkushGrapher-2-Datasets ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15011
- [ADDED] MarkushGlyph and OCSRGlyph (Edison Scientific, 2026; Apache-2.0) are a small-VLM Markush recognizer and an OCSR model, and MarkushGlyph beats MarkushGrapher-2.
  - details: MarkushGlyph is a LoRA (rank 128) on Qwen3.5-2B-Base, trained on a 263K-row index plus 5% OCSR data. It outputs CXSMILES plus an R-group table and scores IP5-M 58.2/60.6 (greedy / majority vote at 8), M2S 61.2/62.1 and USPTO-Markush 59.5/63.5, against MG2 at 53.2, 56.0 and 55.0. OCSRGlyph, a Swin-B encoder with transformer decoder, scores 93.8 on USPTO. Code, weights and datasets are all permissively licensed. This is close to a ready template for the user's 'fine-tune a VLM' plan.
  - sources: https://github.com/EdisonScientific/glyph ; https://huggingface.co/EdisonScientific/MarkushGlyph ; https://huggingface.co/datasets/EdisonScientific/glyph-datasets
- [ADDED] MolSeek-OCR (arXiv 2604.03476, Apr 2026) is a direct negative result on fine-tuning a document-OCR VLM (DeepSeek-OCR-2) for OCSR.
  - details: Full-parameter SFT failed. Training only worked when staged: LoRA first, then selective full fine-tuning with split learning rates and a frozen visual tokenizer, on PubChem renders plus USPTO-MOL. Even then the model only matched the best image-to-sequence models and stayed below image-to-graph models. RL-style post-training (GSPO, ReFT) and data-curation refinement did not improve exact-match SMILES. This is an important caution against plain end-to-end VLM fine-tuning.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.03476 ; hf://papers/2604.03476/paper.md
- [ADDED] Licensing limits many of the strongest open assets to non-commercial use, which matters for drug-discovery work.
  - details: These are CC BY-NC-SA-4.0: UniParser/MolParser-7M, MolGallery (also gated), MolDetv2, MolDet-Bench and OmniScience (gated). The MolRecBench-Wild Hugging Face card declares no license. MinerU.Chem's GTR-VL-1.4.13 is offered only as an online service, with no weights. Permissive options are MarkushGrapher-2 weights (Apache-2.0; code MIT), the Glyph models and data (Apache-2.0), ChemX SAR sets (MIT), and Docling DocumentFigureClassifier (MIT).
  - sources: https://huggingface.co/datasets/UniParser/MolParser-7M ; https://huggingface.co/datasets/UniParser/MolGallery ; https://huggingface.co/UniParser/MolDetv2 ; https://huggingface.co/datasets/opendatalab/MolRecBench-Wild ; https://huggingface.co/docling-project/MarkushGrapher-2
- [ADDED] Many benchmarks are too small for an autoresearch-style keep/discard decision on a single scalar without confidence intervals.
  - details: M2S has 103 test items and USPTO-Markush 74. The public IP5-M subset has 878 items, not the ~1,000 in the paper. RxnScribe-test has 138 images, and the IdtVP authors say small score changes on it lack statistical significance. MolRecBench-Wild subset C has 298 SMILES-evaluable items, and MolMole Patent300 has 300 pages. A pinned validation set like autoresearch's val shard needs either thousands of items (for example the 5,024-image MolRecBench-Wild release plus page-level sets) or a bootstrap-CI acceptance rule.
  - sources: https://huggingface.co/datasets/docling-project/MarkushGrapher-2-Datasets ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15011 ; https://huggingface.co/datasets/opendatalab/MolRecBench-Wild ; https://huggingface.co/datasets/doxa-friend/MolMole_Patent300
- [ADDED] Compute scale clashes with autoresearch's fixed 5-minute experiment budget, so the loop should iterate on small proxy models or sub-tasks.
  - details: Re3-DAPO VLM RL took 870 steps, about 70 hours on 8×H200; the authors could afford only one run. Logics-Parsing GRPO ran 250 steps after a full SFT on >300K pages. By contrast, MolParser-Mobile has 9.98M parameters (1,520 molecules/s on one 4090D), MolDetv2 is a 2.6M YOLO11n, and COMO-style minimum risk training on a Swin encoder-decoder is cheap. These fit fast, comparable fixed-budget experiments, while VLM-scale runs need separate gating.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15011 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19760 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05807 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.23546
- [ADDED] Frontier closed VLMs are now strong zero-shot baselines and are used as teachers or labelers in 2026 chemistry pipelines.
  - details: On revised MolRecBench-Wild, GPT-5.6-Sol (74.87 SMILES), Gemini-3.5-flash-thinking (66.85) and Claude-opus-4-8 (65.47) beat MolScribe (62.29). On reaction parsing, Gemini 3 Pro with identifier prompts zero-shot beats fine-tuned specialists on soft F1. Mid-Mapper (Qwen2.5-VL-3B) is distilled from Gemini 3 Pro outputs, Logics-Parsing used Gemini 2.5 Pro to correct annotations, and Uni-Parser rewrote captions with GPT-5 and Gemini. For the user's data engine, distillation and consensus labels from frontier models are standard practice.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03525 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15011 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.19760 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.15098
- [ADDED] Several ready-made public evaluation sets now exist that can serve as the fixed evaluation set (the role of prepare.py's evaluate function in autoresearch).
  - details: MolRecBench-Wild (HF release 2026-08-19): 5,024 crops from 818 articles, CARBON graph labels, A/B/C difficulty subsets and DOI provenance. MolMole_Patent300 covers page-level patent detection, OCSR and reactions. MarkushGrapher-2-Datasets includes M2S, USPTO-Markush, a public IP5-M subset and a 54.8K USPTO-MOL-M training split. UniParser/MolDet-Bench covers detection. ChemX Oxazolidinones and Benzimidazoles provide SAR records with page and section provenance. Together they cover structure, Markush, page-level and SAR-record extraction.
  - sources: https://huggingface.co/datasets/opendatalab/MolRecBench-Wild ; https://huggingface.co/datasets/doxa-friend/MolMole_Patent300 ; https://huggingface.co/datasets/docling-project/MarkushGrapher-2-Datasets ; https://huggingface.co/datasets/UniParser/MolDet-Bench ; https://huggingface.co/datasets/ai-chem/Oxazolidinones
- [ADDED] DianShi-RxnDB (Shanghai AI Lab, arXiv 2609.06703, Sep 2026) is a fully automated patent text-image-scheme extraction pipeline at USPTO/EPO scale with built-in QC gating.
  - details: It processes ~1.58M USPTO and EPO patents (1976–2025) into ~24M provenance-linked reaction instances, of which 14.8M (61.7%) pass automated qualification checks. On 1,300 sampled records, micro-averaged field accuracy is 92.95%. A matched comparison against Pistachio is included, and the data is served to agents over MCP. The automated QC gate plus source linkage is a template for the bioactivity side of the user's goal.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06703 ; hf://papers/2609.06703/paper.md

### Implications (researcher's recommendations)
- prepare.py (fixed, read-only) should hold a pinned, human-verified, document-level dev set of about 60-120 documents. Split it roughly 50/50 between patents and papers (journal plus SI) and use a temporal cut, e.g. documents published after the base VLM's data cutoff, as RxnCaption did. Stratify by tags such as Markush, chiral, R-group table, cross-page table, footnoted assay conditions, binned activities and scanned pages. Store a version hash, because benchmark revisions alone moved MolScribe from 41 to 62 on MolRecBench-Wild. Never let train.py read these files. Seed the labels from BioVista, DocSAR-200, ChemX Oxazolidinones/Benzimidazoles and BindingDB patent records, then double-review them.
- Define one scalar 'val_recF1' in prepare.py, the analogue of val_bpb: a record-level F1 over (compound, endpoint type, relation, value, unit, target/assay) tuples. Match predicted and gold records with an order-invariant, Hungarian-style assignment, as Re3-DAPO argues for set outputs. A compound matches on RDKit-standardised InChIKey with stereo; use the parent after salt stripping, and score salt form separately. A value matches when its normalised value (molar, compared in log10) lies within |Δ| ≤ 0.05 and the relation (<, >, =, ~, ranges) agrees. Units are normalised against ChEMBL standard_units. The metric must be independent of output format, so CARBON, E-SMILES, CXSMILES and JSON outputs are all canonicalised before scoring.
- Log secondary diagnostics as extra columns in results.tsv but keep/discard only on the scalar. Suggested columns: OCSR exact match on crops, compound-identifier coreference F1, table-row recall (Doc2SAR-style), value/unit/qualifier accuracy, target-linking accuracy, Markush/R-group enumeration F1, chiral-slice accuracy, pages/s and peak VRAM. BioMiner shows the whole gap sits in joining structures with values (joint recall about 0.33, value recall 0.85), so the agent needs these columns to see where an edit helped.
- Adopt a hybrid baseline rather than a pure end-to-end VLM. The evidence favours specialist perception plus VLM reading: OCSR 93 vs 75, table recall 81 vs 29, reaction F1 76 vs 24 with visual prompts. Start the loop from a reference pipeline: MolDetv2 or MolYOLO detection, then a MolParser or GTR-VL-class OCSR, then a VLM (Qwen3-VL or Qwen2.5-VL 3-8B) prompted with identifier or indexed-box visual prompts (IdtVP/BIVP), outputting JSON that refers to molecule indices. Finish with deterministic RDKit R-group enumeration and unit normalisation. Let train.py decide which parts the fine-tuned VLM absorbs, and require any end-to-end variant to beat this baseline on val_recF1.
- Widen what the agent may edit beyond model and optimizer, because MinerU2.5-Pro gained +2.71 points from data alone. Expose in train.py: data-mixture weights (synthetic RDKit/Indigo renders, USPTO CWU image-MOL pairs, MolParser-7M, MolGallery consensus labels, RxnCaption-15k, BindingDB-aligned patent pages), hard-sample mining thresholds (Logics-Parsing used NED 0.5-0.8), output representation (E-SMILES vs CXSMILES vs CARBON), visual-prompt style, image resolution, CoT style (graph-traversal CoT) and the reward mix. Keep dataset downloads and evaluation in prepare.py.
- Five minutes is too short a budget for VLM fine-tuning plus page-level inference. Use a fixed wall-clock budget of roughly 20-30 minutes, for example LoRA or partial fine-tuning of a 2-4B VLM on one H100, with evaluation time excluded and capped by a fixed vLLM batch config. Alternatively, run a two-tier loop: fast screening on a 20-document 'val_small' every run, and the full dev set only for candidate keeps. Log pages/s as a soft constraint, like VRAM, because production systems such as Uni-Parser and MolParser-Mobile treat throughput as first-class.
- Add verifiable-reward options to train.py: an RLVR/GRPO or minimum-risk-training stage whose reward reuses parts of the prepare.py scorer. Candidate signals are RDKit validity, InChIKey exact match, Tanimoto similarity, JSON schema validity and record-level F1 on the training split. olmOCR 2, Re3-DAPO, COMO and Logics-Parsing all gained over SFT this way. Add a chirality-specific reward, since chirality is the weakest OCSR slice (about 0.35-0.65 on BioVista).
- Build cheap, large-scale patent supervision automatically. (a) Align USPTO 'grant full text with embedded TIFF' complex-work-unit MOL/CDX files to their page images to get image-structure pairs; MolScribe used 680K. (b) Align BindingDB patent records (patent number, example number, target, value, relation) to USPTO full text and example tables to get page-to-record pairs. (c) Use ChEMBL document-level activities (doc DOI) for papers. Filter by agreement between several extractors (MolGallery-style unanimity) and keep human-verified data only for evaluation. Expect about 0.3-1 log-unit noise when cross-checking against other assays (Landrum and Riniker), so match values against the source document, not a consensus.
- Encode patent-specific semantics in the target schema from the start. Separate Markush claim scaffolds (CXSMILES plus substituent table) from exemplified compounds (example number, IUPAC name, structure). Resolve 'Example N' references across the synthesis section and the biological-data tables. Record activities reported as bins or ranges as intervals with qualifiers rather than forcing point values. Name-to-structure (OPSIN/LeadMine) is a cheap, high-precision cross-check against OCSR for exemplified compounds; route disagreements to a low-confidence flag rather than silently choosing one.
- Make provenance mandatory in every predicted record: page, bounding box, table and cell coordinates, and footnote ID, as in ChemX, MinerU.Chem and MolMole. Score provenance in a diagnostic column. This lets the human iterating program.md audit failures quickly and lets the loop's 'crash or discard' analysis cluster errors by slice.
- In program.md, encode the simplicity criterion in chemistry terms. A change that adds a new specialist tool or agent for under 0.5 points of val_recF1 is a discard. Removing a pipeline stage (for example, letting the VLM read identifiers directly instead of using a separate linker) at equal F1 is a keep. Forbid any change that reads eval documents or tunes on the BioVista or MolRecBench-Wild test splits. Forbid new heavyweight dependencies beyond RDKit, OPSIN and vLLM.
- Choose the base VLM with chemistry evidence in mind. Frontier and open VLMs read scheme text well (RxnBench fact extraction about 96%) but are weak at structures and bounding-box regression. Prefer bases that support visual-prompt inputs and long JSON output, e.g. Qwen3-VL or Qwen2.5-VL 3-8B as used by RxnCaption, Doc2SAR, Logics-Parsing and MarkushGlyph. Treat OCSR as a tool call or a distilled sub-skill (MolParser-Mobile-style on-policy distillation from a specialist teacher) rather than expecting SFT alone to close an 18-point gap.

### Open questions
- Can a single fine-tuned 3-8B VLM match a specialist OCSR model (about 93% SMILES on MolRecBench-Wild, about 0.80 on BioVista) while also doing table parsing and coreference? Or will the best val_recF1 always come from tool-augmented hybrids? No 2026 paper reports a head-to-head on a document-level SAR benchmark.
- Which scalar should the loop optimise? Strict tuple F1 is the main candidate; alternatives include weighted partial credit (e.g., Tanimoto-weighted structure credit) or separate structure and activity F1s combined by a harmonic mean. How sensitive are keep/discard decisions to this choice?
- How reliable are BindingDB and ChEMBL records as automatic gold labels for patents and papers, given transcription and unit errors and the 80-91% agreement HARVEST reports? How much human re-verification does the pinned dev set need?
- How should Markush claims be scored? Exact CXSMILES match is used by MarkushGrapher; enumerated-set overlap is another option; a third is to restrict scoring to exemplified compounds and treat the claim scaffold as auxiliary.
- Can the fixed time budget (about 20-30 minutes per experiment) produce meaningful signal for VLM LoRA or RL changes on a dev set this small (60-120 documents)? What run-to-run variance should set the minimum 'improvement' threshold?
- Licensing and redistribution: Uni-Parser is CC BY-NC-SA, journal PDFs are mostly non-OA, and some SAR datasets carry publisher restrictions. Which training and eval sources are safe to cache in prepare.py?
- Several 2026 numbers come from search summaries and could not be verified against full text: HARVEST agreement (80% vs 91%), PatSAR (81.86% table recall), ChemX per-field GPT-5 F1, BioMiner Markush-enumeration F1 0.70, and Uni-Parser's Table 4 comparison of PDF tools. They should be re-checked before being used as baselines.
- How well do models trained mainly on English/US patents transfer to CN/JP/KR patents (scanned images, non-Latin text around structures)? IP5-M and Uni-Parser's 85-language layout data suggest the gap is large but unquantified for activity extraction.

## Topic: vlm-finetune-practice

### Summary
As of late 2026, the most practical base models for an autoresearch-style VLM loop are the Apache-2.0 Qwen families. Qwen3-VL comes in 2B/4B/8B/32B dense plus 30B-A3B/235B-A22B MoE, uses 16px patches with 2x2 merging (one token per 32x32 px), and scores OCRBench 858/881/896 for the 2B/4B/8B Instruct models. Qwen3.5 (Feb 2026) comes in 0.8B/2B/4B/9B and up, is natively multimodal, and scores OCRBench 85.0 (4B) and 89.2 (9B), but it thinks by default and uses a hybrid Gated-DeltaNet design that complicates kernels and packing. InternVL3.5, MiniCPM-V 4.5, Kimi-VL, GLM-4.1V/4.5V and Gemma 4 are viable alternatives. Gemma 3 has a fixed 896px / 256-token view and an OCRBench of only 702 at 12B, which makes it a poor fit for dense chemical drawings. General VLMs are still weak at chemistry out of the box. On the 2026 real-literature benchmark MolRecBench-Wild, SMILES exact match is 41% for MolScribe and GTR-Mol-VLM but 30% for Gemini 2.5 Pro, 26% for InternVL3.5-241B and 19-20% for GPT-5 and Intern-S1. Fine-tuned VLMs can match or beat specialists only with careful design: graph-traversal chain of thought, keeping abbreviations as superatoms, real patent images, and reward-based sequence-level training. Direct image-to-SMILES SFT of an OCR VLM (DeepSeek-OCR-2) still trailed MolScribe by 20-30 points. The evidence on RL is mixed. Positive results are olmOCR 2 (unit-test GRPO, +3.9 points), Re3-DAPO for reaction diagrams (+3 to +4 F1 in distribution, but out of distribution one variant collapsed) and COMO's minimum-risk training (+5 to +14 points over MolScribe on real data). GSPO on molecular OCR cut exact match even as graph match rose. RL is expensive: Re3-DAPO took about 70 hours on 8xH200. To limit forgetting, tune the LLM but not the vision encoder, ideally only the attention projections or the MLP gate/up layers, and remember that on-policy RL drifts less than SFT. Most frameworks (LLaMA-Factory, ms-swift, TRL, Unsloth, verl, EasyR1) now support Qwen2.5-VL/Qwen3-VL SFT, and most also support GRPO/DAPO/GSPO, but measured VLM fine-tuning throughput is rarely published. Anchors (MolSight, about 1.5 samples/s per A100-class GPU for an 8B LoRA on molecule images) plus FLOPs arithmetic suggest that on one H100, a 20-30 minute experiment can process roughly 5k-25k molecule crops on an 8B LoRA or several times more on a 2B, but only around 2k-5k full pages. Evaluation with vLLM/SGLang is fast: about 3,050 output tokens/s, or 4.2 pages/s, for a 7B on one H100. On output format, JSON caused more retries and repetition loops than YAML in olmOCR. Constrained decoding with schemas can speed generation by up to 50% and raise accuracy by up to 4%, but strict formats hurt free-form reasoning. Caveat: the web-search budget for this session ran out late in the research. The GTR-VL v3 RL gains, MarkushGrapher-2's exact numbers and some MolParser figures (Markush accuracy, speed) rest on secondary summaries and are marked lower confidence.

### Findings
- [OK] **F1** (existence) Qwen3-VL is an Apache-2.0 family (2B/4B/8B/32B dense; 30B-A3B/235B-A22B MoE) with a SigLIP-2-based encoder, 2x2 MLP token merging, DeepStack and a 256K context; each visual token covers 32x32 px.
  - details: Tech report (arXiv 2511.21631): the encoder is SigLIP-2, further trained at dynamic resolution. A two-layer MLP merges 2x2 visual features into one token. DeepStack injects tokens from three ViT levels into the first three LLM layers. Context is up to 256K. The DeepStack ablation (15B-A2B, 200B tokens) raised DocVQA from 89.5 to 91.1 and OCRBench from 81.0 to 83.6. Pre-training: Stage 0 trains only the merger on 67B tokens; Stage 1 trains all parameters on about 1T tokens at 8K sequence length; later stages use 32K and 262K. GitHub README: patch size 16 for Qwen3-VL vs 14 for Qwen2.5-VL; images are resized to multiples of 32 (28 for Qwen2.5-VL), a 32x spatial compression. Example budgets are 256-1280 tokens per image. OCR covers 32 languages. Instruct and Thinking editions plus FP8 checkpoints exist. HF card for Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct: license apache-2.0, 8.77B params.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.21631 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2511.21631 ; https://github.com/QwenLM/Qwen3-VL ; https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct ; https://raw.githubusercontent.com/QwenLM/Qwen3-VL/main/README.md
- [OK] **F2** (number) Among small Qwen3-VL models, Instruct beats Thinking on OCR: OCRBench 858/881/896 (2B/4B/8B Instruct) vs 792/808/819 (Thinking).
  - details: Qwen3-VL tech report, Table 4 (small models vs GPT-5-nano), Thinking/Instruct pairs. OCRBench (0-1000): 2B 792/858; 4B 808/881; 8B 819/896; GPT-5-nano 753 (high) / 701 (minimal). OmniDocBench-en overall edit distance (lower is better): 2B 0.370/0.292; 4B 0.234/0.244; 8B 0.209/0.170; GPT-5-nano 0.401/0.454. CC-OCR: 2B 68.3/72.8; 4B 73.8/76.2; 8B 76.3/79.9. OCRBench_v2-en: 2B 56.4/56.3; 4B 61.8/63.7; 8B 63.9/65.4. The small Instruct models were evaluated at temperature 1.0, top-p 1.0, top-k 40, presence penalty 2.0.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.21631 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2511.21631
- [OK] **F3** (number) Mid-size and flagship Qwen3-VL OCR scores: OCRBench 903 (30B-A3B Instruct), 895 (32B Instruct) and about 920 (235B-A22B Instruct); OmniDocBench-en edit distance 0.143-0.183.
  - details: Qwen3-VL tech report, Table 3, Thinking/Instruct. 30B-A3B: OCRBench 839/903, OmniDocBench-en 0.165/0.183, OmniDocBench-zh 0.233/0.253, CC-OCR 77.8/80.7. 32B: OCRBench 855/895, OmniDocBench-en 0.148/0.151, CC-OCR 79.6/80.3. Same table, Gemini 2.5 Flash: 853/864. Table 2 (235B-A22B, on a /100 scale): OCRBench 87.5 Thinking / 92.0 Instruct, OmniDocBench-en 0.155/0.143, CC-OCR 81.5/82.2. Same table, Gemini 2.5 Pro: 866/872; GPT-5: 810/787.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.21631 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2511.21631
- [OK] **F4** (number) Qwen3.5 (Feb-Mar 2026) is natively multimodal and Apache-2.0, with 0.8B/2B/4B/9B small models. The 9B scores OCRBench 89.2 and OmniDocBench1.5 87.7, but it thinks by default and uses a hybrid Gated-DeltaNet architecture.
  - details: HF Hub: Qwen/Qwen3.5-0.8B, -2B, -4B and -9B (plus -Base variants) were created 26-28 Feb 2026; 27B, 35B-A3B, 122B-A10B and 397B-A17B appeared from 16 Feb 2026. All are apache-2.0 and tagged image-text-to-text. 9B card: 32 layers laid out as 8 x (3 x Gated DeltaNet + 1 x Gated Attention), context 262,144 natively. Vision-language table (columns: GPT-5-Nano, Gemini-2.5-Flash-Lite, Qwen3-VL-30B-A3B, Qwen3.5-9B, Qwen3.5-4B): OmniDocBench1.5 55.9/79.4/86.8/87.7/86.2; OCRBench 75.3/82.5/83.9/89.2/85.0; CC-OCR 58.9/72.9/77.8/79.3/76.7; CharXiv(RQ) 50.1/56.1/56.6/73.0/70.8. The card says 'Qwen3.5 models operate in thinking mode by default'. TRL docs warn that the hybrid models need flash-linear-attention: otherwise the DeltaNet layers fall back to a PyTorch scan costing about 20x (1046 vs 52 us/token on Qwen3.5-2B). Their recurrent state also carries across padding-free packed rows.
  - sources: https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3.5-9B ; https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3.5-4B ; https://huggingface.co/docs/trl/async_grpo_trainer
- [FIXED] **F5** (number) InternVL3.5 (Aug 2025) pairs InternViT-300M with Qwen3 LLMs from 1B to 14B (InternViT-6B for 38B and 241B-A28B). OCRBench is about 795 (1B), 836 (2B) and 832 (8B). Each 448x448 tile becomes 256 tokens.
  - details: InternVL3.5 paper (arXiv 2508.18265), parameter counts: 1B = InternViT-300M + Qwen3-0.6B (1.1B total); 2B = Qwen3-1.7B (2.3B); 4B = Qwen3-4B (4.7B); 8B = Qwen3-8B (8.5B); 14B; 38B = InternViT-6B + Qwen3-32B; MoE 20B-A4B (GPT-OSS-20B), 30B-A3B and 241B-A28B. OCRBench: 8B 83.2 and 38B 87.0 (ViR/Flash comparison table, where 8B-Flash scores 83.0 with about 50% fewer visual tokens). 1B 795 and 2B 836 come from the paper's table via search snippet. Cascade RL (offline MPO, then online GSPO) gave InternVL3.5-2B +12.2% average on reasoning over its SFT model. MPO is cheap in GPU hours; Cascade RL needs about half the GPU hours of GSPO. Dynamic tiling: 448x448 tiles, pixel shuffle gives 256 tokens per tile, up to 12 tiles in training, plus a thumbnail when there is more than one tile. HF card for OpenGVLab/InternVL3_5-8B: license apache-2.0.
  - CORRECTION: InternVL3.5 Table 4 (OCR/doc benchmarks) gives OCRBench 795 (1B), 836 (2B), 822 (4B), 840 (8B), 836 (14B), 880 (30B-A3B), 870 (38B) and 907 (241B-A28B). The 83.2 / 832 figure for 8B comes only from the ViR/Flash comparison table (8B-Flash 83.0), not from the main OCR table.
  - checker note: Parameter table confirmed: 1B = 0.3B+0.8B (1.1B) through 241B-A28B. Also confirmed: Cascade RL gives +12.2% on reasoning for 2B, about half the GPU hours of GSPO, and 1024 patch tokens pixel-shuffled to 256 per 448 tile. The 8B card is apache-2.0 with 8,528M params.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.18265 ; https://huggingface.co/OpenGVLab/InternVL3_5-8B ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.16821 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2508.18265
- [OK] **F6** (number) OCRBench across mid-size open VLMs: Kimi-VL-A3B 867, Qwen2.5-VL-7B 864, DeepSeek-VL2 811, Llama-3.2-11B 753, Gemma-3-12B 702 (GPT-4o 815).
  - details: Kimi-VL-A3B-Instruct HF card comparison table. OCRBench: GPT-4o 815, GPT-4o-mini 785, Qwen2.5-VL-7B 864, Llama3.2-11B 753, Gemma3-12B-IT 702, DeepSeek-VL2 811, Kimi-VL-A3B 867. InfoVQA: Qwen2.5-VL-7B 82.6, Gemma3-12B 43.8, Kimi-VL 83.2. Activated params (LLM + vision): Qwen2.5-VL-7B 7.6B + 0.7B; Gemma3-12B 12B + 0.4B; DeepSeek-VL2 4.1B + 0.4B (28B total); Kimi-VL 2.8B + 0.4B (16B total, MoonViT native resolution, 128K context, MIT license). Qwen2.5-VL's ViT has about 0.7B params, a non-trivial share of compute for small LLMs.

- [FIXED] **F7** (existence) Gemma 3 resizes images to a fixed 896x896 and condenses each into 256 tokens (with optional Pan&Scan crops), a poor fit for fine chemical detail. Gemma 4 (Mar-May 2026) is Apache-2.0 but has no verified OCR scores here.
  - details: Gemma 3 tech report: a 400M SigLIP encoder, frozen and shared across 4B/12B/27B, takes square 896x896 input. Vision embeddings are condensed to 256 soft tokens. Pan&Scan at inference splits non-square or high-resolution images into 896x896 crops, at extra cost. Gemma 3 uses the Gemma license, not Apache. HF Hub lists Gemma 4 as google/gemma-4-E2B and E4B (2 Mar 2026), 26B-A4B and 31B (11 Mar 2026) and 12B (23 May 2026), all apache-2.0 and image-text-to-text, with paper arXiv 2607.02770. No OCRBench/OmniDocBench numbers for Gemma 4 were verified.
  - CORRECTION: The Gemma 3 details are correct: a frozen 400M SigLIP shared across 4B/12B/27B, fixed 896x896 input, 256 soft tokens, and Pan&Scan; the license is gemma. The Gemma 4 part is wrong. The Gemma 4 model cards do report document-OCR scores, as OmniDocBench 1.5 average edit distance (lower is better): 31B 0.131, 26B-A4B 0.149, 12B Unified 0.164, E4B 0.181, E2B 0.290, vs Gemma 3 27B 0.365. Gemma 4 also supports variable aspect ratio and resolution with visual token budgets of 70/140/280/560/1120, and the card recommends high budgets for OCR and document parsing. So the 'fixed 896 / 256-token' limitation does not carry over to Gemma 4. The 12B 'Unified' model is encoder-free, projecting raw patches linearly; the vision encoder is about 150M on E2B/E4B and about 550M on 26B/31B.
  - checker note: The HF repo creation dates match the finding: E2B/E4B on 2 Mar, 26B-A4B/31B-it on 11 Mar and 12B on 23 May 2026. All are apache-2.0 and cite arXiv 2607.02770.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.19786 ; https://huggingface.co/google/gemma-3-4b-it ; https://huggingface.co/google/gemma-4-31B-it ; https://huggingface.co/google/gemma-4-E4B-it ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2503.19786 ; https://huggingface.co/google/gemma-4-12B-it
