PaddleOCR-VL-1.5 (arXiv 2601.21957) reports 94.50. Licenses on HF: PaddleOCR-VL Apache-2.0 (ERNIE-4.5-0.3B base); MinerU2.5-2509-1.2B AGPL-3.0 (qwen2_vl architecture). These page parsers can serve as front ends that crop figures and tables before chemistry-specific recognition.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.14528 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.21957 ; https://huggingface.co/PaddlePaddle/PaddleOCR-VL ; https://huggingface.co/opendatalab/MinerU2.5-2509-1.2B ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2510.14528 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2601.21957
- [OK] **F10** (number) Measured page-parsing inference throughput on one A100 (OmniDocBench v1.0, batches of 512 PDFs): PaddleOCR-VL 1.35 pages/s with vLLM (1.62 with FastDeploy), MinerU2.5 1.06, MonkeyOCR-pro-1.2B 0.67, dots.ocr 0.35.
  - details: PaddleOCR-VL paper, Table 13, end-to-end from PDF path to Markdown, output tokens counted with cl100k_base. MonkeyOCR-pro-1.2B: vLLM 0.10.2, 0.673 pages/s, 1120 tok/s. dots.ocr: 0.352 pages/s, 533 tok/s. MinerU2.5: 1.057 pages/s, 1648 tok/s. PaddleOCR-VL: SGLang 1.11 pages/s; vLLM 1.345 pages/s, 2068 tok/s; FastDeploy 1.618 pages/s, 2486 tok/s. Full-page evaluation therefore costs about 1 s per page per A100 even for sub-2B models, so a pinned page-level validation set should stay small, around a few hundred pages.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.14528 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2510.14528
- [OK] **F11** (number) olmOCR (v1): a fine-tuned 7B VLM served with SGLang/vLLM reached 3,050 output tokens/s on one H100, about $178 per million pages (about 4.2 pages/s). One training run took 16 node-hours on 8xH100.
  - details: olmOCR paper (arXiv 2502.18443), Table 6: olmOCR on L40S 906 tok/s, 5,697 pages/USD, $176 per million pages; on H100 3,050 tok/s, 5,632 pages/USD, $178 per million pages, at $2.69/h. That works out to about 15,150 pages/h, or roughly 4.2 pages/s per H100, with a measured 12% retry rate. Comparisons: GPT-4o batch $6,240, Mistral OCR $1,000, Gemini Flash 2 batch $249 per million pages. Training: Qwen2-VL-7B-Instruct, full fine-tuning, learning rate 1e-6, AdamW, cosine, 10,000 steps (about 1.2 epochs). One run took 16 node-hours (8xH100); all experiments together took 365 node-hours. Pages rendered at 1024px on the longest edge.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.18443 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2502.18443
- [OK] **F12** (method) olmOCR 2 used GRPO with binary unit-test rewards on synthetic HTML-rendered pages and averaged ('souped') six seeds, raising olmOCR-Bench from 78.5 to 82.4 (±1.1).
  - details: olmOCR 2 (arXiv 2510.19817). Start: Qwen2.5-VL-7B-Instruct after SFT on olmOCR-mix-1025. RL: one epoch on olmOCR2-synthmix-1025 (2,186 PDF pages) on one 8xH100 node with Hugging Face TRL, 28 completions per document, KL beta 0.01. Reward is the fraction of passing unit tests per page (e.g. 4 of 6 passing gives 0.67). Six seeds were trained, three with token-level and three with sequence-level importance sampling, and their weights averaged. Ablation: '+ Synth data, RLVR, souping' moved the total from 78.5 to 82.4 ±1.1. Biggest gains were in math formulas, tables and multi-column layouts. Synthetic pages were generated with claude-sonnet-4 at about $0.12 per page; unit tests derive from the HTML source, so OCR errors by the generator do not corrupt the labels. Input resolution rose from 1024px to 1288px on the longest edge after a sweep. Model released under Apache-2.0.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.19817 ; https://huggingface.co/allenai/olmOCR-2-7B-1025 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2510.19817
- [OK] **F13** (lesson) Output-format choice affects reliability: olmOCR switched its trained output from JSON to YAML and saw far fewer retries and repetition loops at equal benchmark score.
  - details: olmOCR 2 report: 'The first olmOCR was trained to output JSON objects. We switched to YAML, which reduced the retry rate dramatically,' and 'With JSON, we also found more incidences of repetition loops.' Their explanation is that the model no longer has to track open quotes and can emit EOS as soon as it is done. Benchmark scores were unchanged, but inference efficiency improved. DeepSeek-OCR's official vLLM recipe likewise ships an NGramPerReqLogitsProcessor (ngram_size 30, window 90) to suppress repetition, a sign that loop guards are a standard deployment need for OCR VLMs.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.19817 ; https://huggingface.co/deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-OCR ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2510.19817
- [OK] **F14** (lesson) Constrained decoding with JSON Schema can speed generation by up to 50% and raise task accuracy by up to 4%, but engines differ about 2x in schema coverage. Separately, stricter format restrictions measurably hurt LLM reasoning.
  - details: JSONSchemaBench (arXiv 2501.10868) tested 10K real-world schemas on Guidance, Outlines, llama.cpp, XGrammar, OpenAI and Gemini. Findings: (1) constrained decoding can speed generation by 50% over unconstrained decoding (Guidance fast-forwards forced tokens; XGrammar overlaps grammar compilation with prefill; Outlines has high compile time); (2) the best engine supports about twice as many schemas as the worst; (3) constrained decoding improved downstream tasks by up to 4%, even GSM8K. 'Let Me Speak Freely?' (arXiv 2408.02442) found significant reasoning declines under format restrictions, with JSON-mode constrained decoding worst. NL-to-Format, where the model answers freely and then converts, recovers most of the loss, while classification tasks were less affected.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.10868 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.02442 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2501.10868 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2408.02442
- [OK] **F15** (number) On the 2026 real-literature OCSR benchmark MolRecBench-Wild, general and chemistry VLMs are far behind specialists: SMILES exact match is 41.05 for MolScribe and 40.43 for GTR-Mol-VLM, vs 30.06 for Gemini 2.5 Pro, 25.60 for InternVL3.5-241B, 19.68 for GPT-5, 18.98 for Intern-S1, 9.75 for ChemDFM-X and 4.79 for ChemVLM.
  - details: MolRecBench-Wild (arXiv 2605.05832, May 2026): 5,029 structures from 820 recent chemistry papers, labelled with the MOSAIC difficulty framework (37 fine-grained labels; 93.29% of samples carry at least one difficulty label). It adds the CARBON representation for valence variations and icon-based groups, with dual-track SMILES and CARBON evaluation. SMILES / Simplified-Graph / Graph exact match: MolScribe 41.05/34.47; MolNexTR 40.90/34.42; GTR-Mol-VLM 40.43/35.22; DECIMER v2.2 22.84; MolGrapher 20.33/22.81; Gemini 2.5 Pro 30.06/15.67/13.04; Mathpix 27.88; InternVL3.5 25.60/6.88/3.08; GPT-5 19.68/10.00/8.19; Intern-S1 18.98; Seed1.6-Thinking 15.60; Logics-Parsing 15.47; GLM-4.5V 12.13; ChemDFM-X 9.75; GPT-4o 7.94; Qwen-VL-Max 6.95; OCSU 6.06; ChemVLM 4.79. Classic benchmarks (USPTO, UOB) are above 90% for MolGrapher and MolParser, so they no longer separate methods well.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05832 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2605.05832 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2608.03525
- [FIXED] **F16** (method) GTR-Mol-VLM (renamed GTR-VL in v3, Jan 2026) recipe: full fine-tuning of Qwen2.5-VL-3B on 1.3M graph-traversal chain-of-thought samples at peak LR 1.6e-4, effective batch 1024, 3 epochs, 32xA100 with ZeRO-2. Keeping abbreviations as superatoms gave a 9-14 point gain on abbreviated molecules.
  - details: Paper arXiv 2506.07553. The output is a depth-first atom/bond traversal ('graph traversal as visual CoT') followed by the SMILES. 'Faithfully recognize what you've seen': abbreviations such as Ph, Et and Boc stay as superatoms instead of being expanded, so images and labels agree; a correction pipeline turned USPTO-680K into 351K cleaned samples. Data is GTR-CoT-1.3M (PubChem 1M plus USPTO 351K). Training: AdamW, cosine schedule with 10% warmup, 2 per GPU x 16 accumulation steps x 32 GPUs = 1024 effective batch, all parameters updated. On MolRec-Abb, v1 reports about +14 points over the second-best model and v2 says +9 points on all three metrics (Gen_SMILES, Gra_SMILES, Graph). Retraining MolScribe and MolNexTR on GTR-CoT-1.3M also helped them a lot, but the VLM stayed ahead. v3 adds GRPO with format, graph and SMILES rewards for hand-drawn images that only have SMILES labels; the exact RL gains were not verified here because the web-search budget ran out.
  - CORRECTION: Confirmed from v2 (arXiv 2506.07553v2): Qwen2.5-VL-3B ('Qwen-VL 2.5 3B') full fine-tuning; 32 A100s; AdamW; peak LR 1.6e-4; cosine schedule with 10% warmup; 2/GPU x 16 accumulation = effective batch 1024; all params; ZeRO-2; 3 epochs; GTR-CoT-1.3M = PubChem-1M (Indigo-rendered) + USPTO-351K cleaned from 680K. v2 text states about 14 percentage points over the second-best model on abbreviated-group images (SMILES and graph metrics). The '+9 points' attribution to v2, the rename to GTR-VL in a Jan-2026 v3, and the v3 GRPO stage could not be verified. The HF paper page still carries the title 'GTR-CoT', and the v2 Limitations section only proposes RL as future work.
  - checker note: The core recipe is verified; the version-history and RL claims are unverifiable in this session.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07553 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2506.07553 ; https://github.com/opendatalab/GTR-CoT
- [OK] **F17** (lesson) Fine-tuning DeepSeek-OCR-2 (an OCR-specialized VLM) for image-to-SMILES (MolSeek-OCR, Apr 2026): direct full SFT failed. Two stages (LoRA, then selective full FT) reached only DECIMER-level accuracy (USPTO 61.0 vs MolScribe 92.6), and GSPO/ReFT post-training lowered exact match.
  - details: arXiv 2604.03476. Stage 1: LoRA rank 64, alpha 64, dropout 0.1, on attention, FFN and vision-language projector layers; LR 2e-4, weight decay 1e-3, 100 warmup steps, batch 4 x accumulation 8, 3000 steps on about 96K-192K samples (PubChem renders in MolScribe-like and ChemDraw-like styles plus USPTO-MOL). Stage 2: 800K samples (300K + 300K + 200K), freezing the lowest visual tokenizer and input embeddings. Split LRs: base 1e-5, vision branch 5e-6, language branch 2e-5; 2500 steps. Exact match (zero-shot base was about 0 everywhere): MolSeek-OCR Indigo 74.3, ChemDraw 72.2, CLEF 63.3, UOB 72.6, USPTO 61.0, Staker 50.5, ACS 29.9. MolScribe: 97.5/93.8/88.9/87.9/92.6/86.9/71.9. LoRA-only reached 50.6 on USPTO. GSPO with a chemistry-aware reward (validity, sequence match, chirality-insensitive graph match, stereo handling) raised graph-level accuracy slightly but reduced SMILES exact match, so it was dropped.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.03476 ; https://github.com/HaCTang/MolSeek-OCR ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2604.03476 ; https://huggingface.co/deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-OCR-2
- [OK] **F18** (method) COMO (Apr 2026) adds minimum-risk training (sequence-level, sampled, reward-weighted) to a MolScribe-style OCSR model and sets the best exact match on all 10 benchmarks. The reward is 0.1·valid + 0.5·similarity + 0.4·exact.
  - details: arXiv 2604.23546. MRT loss = sum_i Q_alpha(y_i|x)·(1−R), over N=32 multinomial samples at temperature 0.5, with sharpness alpha 1.0 and weight lambda 0.1. It is interleaved with MLE: 5 MLE-only warmup epochs, then one MRT step every K MLE steps, with gradients summed. This interleaving beat a two-stage MLE-then-MRT schedule. Similarity can be normalized Levenshtein distance, Morgan-fingerprint Tanimoto, or cosine between rendered images from a contrastive 'MolDiscriminator'; all three land within about 2 points of each other. Data: 1M PubChem + 652K USPTO for MLE; 83K MolParser-SFT real images for MRT, which needs only SMILES labels, not coordinates. Exact match for COMO-Tanimoto: Indigo 98.6, ChemDraw 96.5, CLEF 94.8, JPO 88.4, UOB 98.0, USPTO 93.4, USPTO-10K 96.1, Staker 87.4, ACS 84.6, WildMol-10K 77.1, versus MolScribe's 76.2 on JPO and 66.4 on WildMol-10K. Ablation: training on the same 83K images with MLE instead of MRT degraded 5 of 10 benchmarks. MRT applied to SwinOCSR gave 2-20x gains on real data. Training used 4xL40, LR 4e-4, 30 epochs.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.23546 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2604.23546
- [FIXED] **F19** (number) MolSight (Jul 2026), built on Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct with topology and grounding modules and LoRA fine-tuning, reaches 0.81 exact accuracy (Tanimoto 0.998) on PubChem image-to-SMILES, while generalist VLMs score about 0. Stereochemistry is the main remaining error.
  - details: arXiv 2607.01982. The image-to-SMILES table (baselines from ChemMLLM) reports average Tanimoto / accuracy / validity. MolScribe 0.98/0.66/96.9%; DECIMER 0.97/0.78/99.9%; Qwen-VL-7B 0.08/0.0/8.2%; InternVL-20B 0.09/0.0; GPT-4o 0.29/0.01/74.5%; MolSight-8B 0.998/0.81/99.7%; MolSight-32B 0.999/0.83. Non-stereo accuracy reaches 0.9892 for MolSight-32B; stereo accuracy is much lower. Stage 2 (SMILES translation): vision encoder frozen, LoRA on the LLM (90.4M trainable params), LR 2e-4, batch 2/GPU x accumulation 8, 2 epochs over 95K samples, about 18 hours on 2 A800/A100-80GB GPUs. That is about 2.9 samples/s in total, or about 1.5 samples/s per GPU. Note this test set is clean PubChem renders, not real literature.
  - CORRECTION: The numbers are right: on PubChem image-to-SMILES (baselines from ChemMLLM), MolSight-8B scores 0.998 / 0.81 / 99.7% and MolSight-32B 0.999 / 0.83, vs MolScribe 0.98 / 0.66 and DECIMER 0.97 / 0.78. Stage 2 uses 90.4M LoRA params, LR 2e-4, 2 GPUs, about 18 h. The finding misses a decisive caveat: MolSight is not image-only OCSR. It takes as a second input an RDKit-generated SVG of the same molecule, which encodes atom symbols, bond primitives and 2D coordinates, fused through cross-attention ('our topology adaptation layer requires SVG input'). An SVG-only ablation without the image already reaches 0.46 accuracy. The 0.81 is therefore not comparable with MolScribe/DECIMER and does not transfer to raster figures in papers or patents, where no SVG exists. Also, a different, earlier OCSR model named 'MolSight' (EfficientViT, MolScribe-like, pretrained on MolParser-7M) appears in COMO's tables with WildMol-10K 9.0. The two should not be conflated.
  - checker note: Adaptive-protocol figures: Stereo ACC 0.48 (8B) / 0.53 (32B) vs Non-stereo ACC 0.984 / 0.989. Stereochemistry is the main remaining error, as the finding states.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01982 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2607.01982 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2604.23546
- [OK] **F20** (existence) MolParser (ICCV 2025) introduced E-SMILES, an RDKit-compatible extended SMILES covering Markush structures, attachment points, abstract rings and polymers, and the MolParser-7M dataset, which is licensed CC-BY-NC-SA-4.0. MolParser-Base reaches 76.9% on WildMol-10K.
  - details: arXiv 2411.11098 (DP Technology). Architecture: Swin encoder plus BART decoder, trained by curriculum from synthetic to real data. About 400K human-annotated real crops from patents and papers were selected by active learning. WildMol benchmark: 10K ordinary and 10K Markush structures. Exact match on WildMol-10K as tabulated in COMO: Tiny 73.1, Small 76.3, Base 76.9. JPO 75.6-78.9; USPTO-10K 89.5-94.8. Two figures come only from a third-party (alphaXiv) summary: 38.1% on WildMol-10K-M (Markush) and up to 130 images/s. COMO notes MolParser was not open-source when it wrote its paper. On the HF Hub, the dataset UniParser/MolParser-7M has about 7.7M synthetic pretraining pairs plus SFT and real subsets, under license cc-by-nc-sa-4.0, so commercial use is restricted. A compact successor, MolParser-Mobile (arXiv 2609.05807, Sep 2026), targets ultrafast large-scale mining.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.11098 ; https://huggingface.co/datasets/UniParser/MolParser-7M ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.23546 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05807 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2411.11098 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2604.23546 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2609.05807
- [OK] **F21** (existence) Markush structures remain hard. MarkushGrapher-2 (IBM/ETH, CVPR 2026) jointly encodes OCR text, image and layout and outputs a backbone graph plus a substituent table; on the new IP5-M benchmark it reportedly gives exact CXSMILES 53.7% of the time.
  - details: arXiv 2603.28550 (Mar 2026). Pipeline: a dedicated chemical OCR step, then a Vision-Text-Layout encoder plus an OCSR vision encoder, then autoregressive generation of (1) the Markush backbone graph and (2) a table of R-group substituents. Training data comes from an automatic pipeline over USPTO MOL files and images. Benchmark IP5-M: 1,000 manually annotated Markush structures from five major patent offices. The 53.7% exact-CXSMILES figure comes from a third-party (TPR International) review and was not verified in the paper because the search budget ran out. The predecessor MarkushGrapher (arXiv 2503.16096) used the same text, image and layout idea. Implication: patent Markush needs both image and text context, plus a structured core-plus-R-table output, not a single SMILES.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.28550 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16096 ; https://www.tprinternational.com/?p=2930 ; https://github.com/DS4SD/MarkushGrapher ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2503.16096
- [OK] **F22** (number) Verifiable-reward RL (Re3-DAPO, Mar 2026) on chemical reaction-diagram parsing gave +3 to +4 F1 in distribution over SFT but cost about 70 hours on 8xH200. One prompt variant collapsed out of distribution into repetition loops.
  - details: arXiv 2603.15011. The SFT cold start uses about 10% of the data (stopping where loss plateaus) to teach the JSON schema; ms-swift, 8xA100, LR 1e-5, effective batch 128, ZeRO-2. DAPO in verl on 8xH200: LR 1e-6, global batch 256, FSDP, clip [0.2, 0.28], KL 0.01, N=8 rollouts, 870 steps (about 5 epochs), about 70 hours. Unparseable or invalid JSON gets reward 0. The reward is the mean of hybrid-match and soft-match reaction-level F1, an order-invariant set match that removes serialization-order noise. Hybrid F1 on RxnCaption-15k-test: RxnID (IdtVP) SFT 61.2 to 64.4 with RL; RxnCaption (BIVP) 59.8 to 64.1. On the out-of-distribution ScannedRxn set, IdtVP+RL went from 54.5 to 56.3, but BIVP+RL fell from 51.0 to 45.1 because of degenerate token repetition. Zero-shot, Qwen2.5-VL-7B scored only 4.9 hybrid F1 and Gemini 3 Pro 58.3.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15011 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2603.15011
- [OK] **F23** (number) Document-level structure-activity extraction remains hard end to end: Doc2SAR reports 80.78% strict Table Recall on DocSAR-200, 51.48 points above end-to-end GPT-4o (about 29.3%).
  - details: Doc2SAR (arXiv 2506.21625, DP Technology, Jun 2025, revised Oct 2025) is a synergistic pipeline that combines OCSR with a multimodal LLM for linking molecules to activity tables. DocSAR-200 has 200 documents with jointly annotated molecular structures and activity tables. Table Recall counts a row correct only if the molecule's SMILES and all linked activity values are right. Zero-shot baselines included GPT-4o, Gemini-2.5-flash, Llama3.2-VL-90B and Qwen2.5-VL-72B/7B. The benchmark and scripts were to be released at github.com/Jesse-zjx/Doc2SAR. Per-model table values were not retrieved. Related: BioChemInsight (arXiv 2504.10525) extracts structures and activities from patents.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21625 ; https://github.com/Jesse-zjx/Doc2SAR ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.10525
- [OK] **F24** (method) Layout-aware RL for document parsing (Infinity-Parser) used GRPO on Qwen2.5-VL-7B with a composite verifiable reward (normalized edit distance, paragraph-count accuracy, reading order). Its authors find SFT plateaus on page-level accuracy where RL keeps improving.
  - details: Infinity-Parser (arXiv 2506.03197) uses verl on 8xA100-80GB, KL beta 0.01, 8 sampled responses per instance, and its Infinity-Doc-400K dataset (about 400K scanned documents, synthetic plus real). The paper reports that SFT keeps strong paragraph-level accuracy but its page-level (structure plus order) accuracy stops improving as data grows, while LayoutRL improves page-level accuracy and generalizes out of distribution. A follow-up, Infinity-Parser2 (arXiv 2607.07836, Jul 2026), uses multi-task RL with controllable data synthesis. Perception-R1 (arXiv 2504.07954) found on Qwen2-VL-2B that explicit chain of thought is unnecessary or even harmful for perception RL, that reward design is pivotal, and that RL beats SFT more on complex perception (+4.2 F1 on PageOCR, +17.9% on PixMo-Count).
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03197 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07836 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.07954 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2506.03197 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2607.07836 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2504.07954
- [OK] **F25** (number) Which parts to tune: updating the LLM is what teaches new skills (+31.8 learning / −23.3 forgetting), tuning the vision encoder helps little and hurts (+9.6/−10.8), and tuning only attention projections (+24.9/−0.6) or only MLP gate/up (+30.5/−2.1) learns almost as much with little forgetting.
  - details: 'How to Teach Large Multimodal Models New Skills' (arXiv 2510.08564, Oct 2025): sequential fine-tuning on five skills (including OCR, counting and medical VQA) with eight held-out benchmarks (including DocVQA, InfoVQA, MMStar, ScienceQA), averaged over three task orders. Tested on LLaVA-OneVision, with trends confirmed on LLaVA-NeXT and Qwen2.5-VL. Deltas are relative learning on target tasks and forgetting on held-out tasks. Apparent forgetting partly recovers in later stages and tracks a shift in the output-token distribution, measurable with a simple counting-bias probe. Recommended recipes: tune only self-attention Q/K/V/O, or MLP gate+up with down frozen. It also cites earlier work showing LoRA both learns less and forgets less than full fine-tuning.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.08564 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2510.08564
- [OK] **F26** (lesson) On-policy RL forgets less than SFT at equal new-task performance, because forgetting scales with the KL shift from the base policy on the new task ('RL's Razor', ICLR 2026).
  - details: Shenfeld, Pari and Agrawal (MIT), arXiv 2509.04259. Across LLM and robotics foundation-model experiments, the amount of forgetting is predicted by the KL divergence between the fine-tuned and base policy on the new-task distribution. On-policy RL is implicitly biased toward KL-minimal solutions, while SFT can converge arbitrarily far from the base. Specific benchmark numbers were not verified here. The COMO ablation agrees qualitatively: adding real images through MLE caused slight forgetting on 5 of 10 OCSR benchmarks, while sequence-level MRT on the same data did not.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04259 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.23546 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2509.04259
- [OK] **F27** (method) Qwen's official VLM fine-tuning recipe is conservative: full fine-tuning at LR 2e-7 to 1e-6, projector LR 1e-5, vision-tower LR 1e-6, vision tower frozen by default, small max_pixels, and optional data flattening or packing.
  - details: QwenLM/Qwen3-VL repo, qwen-vl-finetune (covers Qwen2/2.5-VL and Qwen3-VL). The README says 'the suggested learning rate is from 1e-6 to 2e-7'; its example uses mm_projector_lr 1e-5, vision_tower_lr 1e-6, tune_mm_llm True / tune_mm_vision False / tune_mm_mlp False, per-device batch 4 x accumulation 4, warmup 0.03, cosine, weight decay 0.01, bf16. Image max_pixels 576*28*28 (≈576 tokens) and min 16*28*28 for Qwen2.5-VL. scripts/sft.sh: LR 2e-7, ZeRO-3, gradient checkpointing, max_pixels 50176 (64 tokens), model_max_length 8192, 0.5 epochs. Other notes: data_flatten concatenates a batch into one sequence; data_packing needs tools/pack_data.py; FlashAttention-2 needs flash_attn 2.7.4.post1; 'If trained with both image and video data, tune_mm_vision should be False'; Qwen3-VL MoE does not support ZeRO-3; 'Training resolution is critical for the model performances.'
  - sources: https://github.com/QwenLM/Qwen3-VL/tree/main/qwen-vl-finetune ; https://raw.githubusercontent.com/QwenLM/Qwen3-VL/main/qwen-vl-finetune/scripts/sft.sh
- [FIXED] **F28** (number) Hyperparameters used in published chemistry and document VLM fine-tunes span about 3 orders of magnitude: LoRA 1e-4 to 2e-4, selective or full fine-tuning 5e-6 to 2e-5, olmOCR full fine-tuning 1e-6, RL 1e-6 to 1e-5, with one outlier full fine-tune at 1.6e-4 (effective batch 1024).
  - details: Sources: MolSight LoRA 2e-4 (stage 2), 1e-4 and 5e-5 (stage-1 module training), warmup 0.03, weight decay 0.01, clip 1.0. MolSeek-OCR LoRA r64 2e-4, then split full fine-tuning at 5e-6 (vision) and 2e-5 (language). RxnCaption cold-start full SFT 1e-5 in ms-swift; Re3-DAPO RL 1e-6. olmOCR full fine-tuning 1e-6, effective batch 4 per device. GTR-Mol-VLM full fine-tuning of Qwen2.5-VL-3B at 1.6e-4 with effective batch 1024 and 10% warmup. TRL's GRPO VLM example: Qwen2.5-VL-3B, LR 1e-5, LoRA on q_proj/v_proj, vLLM colocate. Qwen official full fine-tuning: 2e-7 to 1e-6. The variance suggests LR is a high-value knob for an automated search, scaled to batch size and to which modules are trainable.
  - CORRECTION: Same picture, with one detail fixed: olmOCR v1 used full fine-tuning at LR 1e-6 with an effective batch size of 4 in total, not 4 per device. The other anchors check out: MolSight LoRA 2e-4 (stage-1 modules 1e-4 and 5e-5, warmup 0.03, wd 0.01, clip 1.0); MolSeek split LRs; Re3-DAPO RL 1e-6 with a 1e-5 SFT stage in ms-swift; GTR full fine-tuning 1.6e-4 at batch 1024; TRL GRPO VLM example 1e-5 with LoRA on q_proj/v_proj and vLLM colocate; Qwen official 2e-7 to 1e-6. That spans about 3 orders of magnitude.
  - checker note: The conclusion that LR, scaled to batch size and the set of trainable modules, is the highest-value search knob is reasonable.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01982 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.03476 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15011 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.18443 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07553 ; https://huggingface.co/docs/trl/grpo_trainer ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2607.01982 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2604.03476 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2603.15011 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2502.18443 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2506.07553
- [OK] **F29** (existence) Framework support (late 2026): LLaMA-Factory and ms-swift cover nearly all target VLMs for SFT/LoRA/QLoRA/DPO. ms-swift, verl and EasyR1 support GRPO-family RL for VLMs. TRL's GRPOTrainer supports VLMs (with vLLM colocate or server mode) but its new async trainer does not yet take images.
  - details: LLaMA-Factory lists Qwen3-VL 2B-235B, Qwen2.5-VL, InternVL 2.5-3.5 (1B-241B), MiniCPM-V 4.5/4.6, GLM-4.5(6)V, GLM-4.1V, Kimi-VL, Gemma 3/3n, PaliGemma2, Llama 3.2 Vision, Pixtral, Intern-S1-mini and more. Its training modes are pre-training, SFT, RM, PPO, DPO, KTO, ORPO and SimPO, each with full, freeze, LoRA, QLoRA or OFT. Flags: flash_attn fa2, use_unsloth, enable_liger_kernel, neat_packing. ms-swift claims 300+ to 400+ MLLMs, including Qwen3-VL, InternVL3.5, MiniCPM-V-4, GLM4.5-V and DeepSeek-VL2. Its GRPO family (GRPO, DAPO, GSPO, SAPO, CISPO, CHORD, RLOO, Reinforce++) is multimodal-capable, it has a Megatron backend, and it claims multimodal packing speeds training by '100%+'. TRL v1.15: GRPOTrainer tested on Gemma3, LLaVA-NeXT, Qwen2-VL, Qwen2.5-VL and SmolVLM2. KL beta defaults to 0.0. Loss variants include dapo, dr_grpo, sequence-level importance sampling (GSPO) and SAPO. AsyncGRPOTrainer only handles text-only data for VLMs and freezes the vision tower. verl: VLM RL for Qwen2.5-VL and Kimi-VL with PPO, GRPO, GSPO, DAPO, DrGRPO, RLOO, REINFORCE++ and more; vLLM/SGLang rollouts; multi-GPU LoRA RL. EasyR1: Qwen2/2.5/3-VL with GRPO, DAPO, GSPO, CISPO, RLOO and Reinforce++.
  - sources: https://github.com/hiyouga/LLaMA-Factory ; https://github.com/modelscope/ms-swift ; https://huggingface.co/docs/trl/grpo_trainer ; https://huggingface.co/docs/trl/async_grpo_trainer ; https://github.com/volcengine/verl ; https://github.com/hiyouga/EasyR1
- [OK] **F30** (number) Memory anchors: LoRA or freeze-tuning a 7B needs about 16GB, pure-bf16 full fine-tuning about 60GB and mixed-precision full fine-tuning about 120GB (LLaMA-Factory estimates). EasyR1 estimates GRPO for a 3B VLM fits on 1x40GB (bf16 full) or 1x24GB (LoRA), and a 7B needs 4x40GB (bf16 full) or 2x32GB (LoRA).
  - details: LLaMA-Factory estimates (rule of thumb in GB for an x-billion-parameter model): full AMP 18x (7B: 120GB); full pure_bf16 8x (60GB); freeze/LoRA/GaLore 2x (16GB); QLoRA 8-bit x (10GB); QLoRA 4-bit x/2 (6GB). EasyR1 GRPO table: full fine-tuning AMP 1.5B 2x24GB, 3B 4x40GB, 7B 8x40GB, 32B 16x80GB; full BF16 1.5B 1x24GB, 3B 1x40GB, 7B 4x40GB, 32B 8x80GB, 72B 16x80GB; LoRA AMP 1.5B 1x12GB, 3B 1x24GB, 7B 2x32GB, 32B 2x80GB. These exclude long image-token sequences, which add activation memory (mitigated by gradient checkpointing and Liger fused cross-entropy). So on one 80GB H100: full fine-tuning SFT of 2B-4B and LoRA SFT of 8B are comfortable, 8B full fine-tuning SFT is tight, and RL is realistic only for 2B-4B (full or LoRA) or 7B-8B with LoRA.
  - sources: https://github.com/hiyouga/LLaMA-Factory ; https://github.com/hiyouga/EasyR1
- [OK] **F31** (number) Vendor speed claims: Liger kernels give +20% multi-GPU throughput and −60% memory (up to −80% with fused linear cross-entropy). Unsloth claims Qwen3-VL trains 1.7x faster with 60% less VRAM, and VLM GRPO/GSPO 1.5-2x faster with 90% less VRAM. LoRA adapters slow vLLM generation by 1.33-1.39x per token.
  - details: Liger-Kernel README: '+20% throughput, −60% memory' measured on LLaMA-3-8B, batch 8, bf16, FSDP1 on 8xA100. Fused linear kernels cut memory up to 80%. Multimodal patching covers Qwen2-VL, Qwen2.5-VL, Qwen3.5 MoE, InternVL3, Gemma3, Gemma4, Llama 3.2-Vision, Llama4, Pixtral and PaliGemma; Qwen3-VL was not listed. Unsloth docs (vendor, baselines unspecified): Qwen3-VL '1.7x faster with 60% less VRAM and 8x longer context'; VLM RL '1.5-2x faster, 90% less VRAM, 15x longer context'. LLaMA-Factory reports Unsloth LoRA at 170% speed (Dec 2023, LLM). TRL async GRPO docs: vLLM serving a LoRA adapter generates 1.33-1.39x slower per token than merged weights; merging a 7B and syncing it costs about 15GB per sync vs about 160MB for an r=32 adapter.
  - sources: https://github.com/linkedin/Liger-Kernel ; https://docs.unsloth.ai/models/qwen3-vl-run-and-fine-tune ; https://unsloth.ai/docs/new/vision-reinforcement-learning-vlm-rl ; https://huggingface.co/docs/trl/async_grpo_trainer ; https://github.com/hiyouga/LLaMA-Factory
- [FIXED] **F32** (number) Vision-token arithmetic (derived from patch and merge sizes): a 512x512 molecule crop costs about 256 tokens in Qwen3-VL (about 324 in Qwen2.5-VL); a 1288x1000 page costs about 1,240 (Qwen3-VL) or about 1,656 (Qwen2.5-VL); InternVL costs 256 per 448px tile plus a thumbnail; Gemma 3 is a fixed 256 per 896px crop.
  - details: Derived from F1, F5, F7 and F27. Qwen2.5-VL: one token per 28x28 px (14px patch, 2x2 merge). Qwen3-VL: one token per 32x32 px (16px patch, 2x2 merge). Examples: 448x448 → Qwen2.5-VL 256, Qwen3-VL 196, InternVL 256 (one tile); 512x512 → about 324 / 256; 1024x1024 → about 1,296 / 1,024 / InternVL 5x256 = 1,280 (2x2 tiles plus thumbnail); a 1288-px longest-edge letter page (olmOCR 2's chosen size) → about 46x36 = 1,656 (Qwen2.5-VL) or about 40x31 = 1,240 (Qwen3-VL); an A4 page at 200 DPI (1654x2339) → about 4,956 or 3,796 tokens uncapped. The ViT runs on 4x as many patches as merged tokens; Qwen2.5-VL-7B's ViT is about 0.7B params. Other encoders: DeepSeek-OCR modes Tiny 512 / Small 640 / Base 1024 / Large 1280 / Gundam (1024 global plus 640 crops), and the authors report 97% decoding precision below 10x text-to-vision compression vs about 60% at 20x. MiniCPM-V uses 64 tokens per 448px slice, up to 1.8MP. Lever: max_pixels bounds sequence length and so dominates per-sample cost and the batch size that fits.
  - CORRECTION: Two arithmetic errors. (1) Qwen2.5-VL rounds each side to the nearest multiple of 28, so a 1024x1024 image becomes 37x37 = 1,369 tokens, not about 1,296. (2) InternVL dynamic tiling with the default max_num=12 maps a 1024x1024 image to a 3x3 grid, because the area tie-break prefers larger grids when the image area exceeds 0.5·448²·9. That gives 9 tiles plus a thumbnail = 10 x 256 = 2,560 tokens, not 1,280; 1,280 applies only if max_num ≤ 8. These values are correct: 512² → 324 (Q2.5) / 256 (Q3); 448² → 256 / 196; 1288x1000 → 1,656 / 1,240; A4 at 200 DPI → 4,956 / 3,796; Gemma 3 fixed at 256 per 896 crop; DeepSeek-OCR modes Tiny 512 to Gundam. Gemma 4 instead uses selectable budgets of 70-1120 tokens per image.
  - checker note: For reference, olmOCR v1 reports about 1,000 image tokens per page at 1024px with Qwen2-VL.
  - sources: https://github.com/QwenLM/Qwen3-VL ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.21631 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.16821 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.19786 ; https://huggingface.co/deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-OCR ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.18234 ; https://huggingface.co/openbmb/MiniCPM-V-4_5 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2404.16821 ; https://huggingface.co/OpenGVLab/InternVL3_5-8B ; https://huggingface.co/google/gemma-4-31B-it
- [UNVERIFIED] **F33** (number) Training-throughput estimate for one H100 (derived; low to medium confidence): about 3-13 molecule crops/s for an 8B LoRA, about 10-45/s for a 2B, and about 1-3 full pages/s for an 8B LoRA. That is roughly 5k-25k crops (8B) or 20k-80k crops (2B) per 30-minute run, but only about 2k-5k pages.
  - details: Measured anchors: MolSight, Qwen3-VL-8B LoRA on molecule images, about 190K samples in about 18 hours on 2 A800/A100 GPUs, about 1.5 samples/s per GPU, with small batches and extra modules (F19). nanoVLM-222M: about 1.7M samples in about 6 hours on one H100 (about 79 samples/s). olmOCR v1: one 7B full fine-tuning run took 16 node-hours on 8xH100 (F11). FLOPs model: LoRA ≈ 6·N·T (forward, input-gradient backward and checkpoint recompute; no weight gradients for frozen base) plus a frozen ViT forward ≈ 2·N_vit·4·T_vis. A crop with 256 vision + 100 prompt + 80 answer tokens on an 8B model costs about 2.2e13 FLOPs. H100 bf16 dense peak is 989 TFLOPS; typical HF-Trainer MFU on short, variable-length VLM batches is about 5-30%. That gives about 2-13 samples/s, consistent with the MolSight anchor scaled about 2-3x from A100 to H100. 2B models are about 3.5x cheaper per token. Full pages (about 1,250 vision + 800 output tokens) are about 5x costlier per sample than crops. Packing and FlashAttention-2 sit at the high end of these ranges; the 100%+ packing speedup is ms-swift's claim. Calibrate with a 60-second benchmark logging samples/s and tokens/s before fixing the budget.
  - checker note: This is a derived estimate, not a published measurement. The anchors check out. MolSight stage 2 ran 5,938 steps x 2 x 8 x 2 GPUs ≈ 190K samples in about 18 h, i.e. about 1.47 samples/s per GPU; that run also encodes SVG tokens, so it is not a pure image-to-text cost. nanoVLM-222M saw 1.7M samples in about 6 h on one H100. The FLOP arithmetic is consistent: 6·8e9·436 ≈ 2.1e13 plus ViT. The 5-30% MFU range is an assumption. Measure throughput before fixing the time budget, as the finding itself recommends.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01982 ; https://huggingface.co/lusxvr/nanoVLM-222M ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.18443 ; https://github.com/modelscope/ms-swift ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2607.01982 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2502.18443
- [UNVERIFIED] **F34** (number) Batched evaluation is cheap compared with training: a 7B VLM serves about 3,050 output tokens/s on one H100 with SGLang/vLLM. For SMILES-length outputs (about 60-120 tokens), that implies roughly 25-50 crops/s for 7-8B, and more for 2-4B, so a 1-2K-crop validation set takes about 1 minute.
  - details: Anchors: olmOCR on H100, 3,050 tok/s, about 4.2 pages/s (F11). PaddleOCR-VL-0.9B 1.35 pages/s and MinerU2.5 1.06 pages/s on A100 (F10). DeepSeek-OCR authors claim 200k+ pages/day on one A100-40G (author claim, not independently verified; a heise.de hands-on found single-page latency much higher on an RTX 4090). Derived: crops are dominated by prefill of about 300-400 tokens plus about 100 decode tokens, so decode-bound throughput is about 3,050/100 ≈ 30 crops/s for a 7B. Use greedy decoding, cap max_tokens and use prefix caching off or on as the model recipe recommends. Constrained decoding (XGrammar or Guidance) can further cut time per output token (F14).
  - checker note: This is a derived estimate. The 3,050 tok/s anchor is olmOCR's full-page workload (about 3,000-token prompts, long outputs), so dividing by about 100 output tokens is a rough heuristic. Batched crops with short outputs are likely prefill- and ViT-bound, and could be faster or slower than 30/s depending on max_pixels. DeepSeek-OCR's '200k+ pages/day on one A100-40G' is an author claim. The heise hands-on was not checked.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.18443 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.14528 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.18234 ; https://heise.de/-10851315 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2502.18443 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2510.14528 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2510.18234
- [OK] **F35** (license) Licensing traps among chemistry and document assets: ChemDFM-X is AGPL-3.0, MinerU2.5 is AGPL-3.0, the MolParser-7M data is CC-BY-NC-SA-4.0 and Gemma 3 is under the Gemma license. ChemVLM, Intern-S1/S1-mini, PaddleOCR-VL, olmOCR-2, Qwen3-VL, Qwen3.5, InternVL3.5, MiniCPM-V 4.5 and Gemma 4 are Apache-2.0; DeepSeek-OCR, GLM-4.1V/4.5V, Kimi-VL and Phi-4-multimodal are MIT.
  - details: HF Hub metadata: OpenDFM/ChemDFM-X-v1.0-13B (13.8B, llama arch) agpl-3.0; AI4Chem/ChemVLM-26B-1-2 (25.5B, internvl_chat) apache-2.0; internlm/Intern-S1 (240.7B total MoE, about 28B active, 5T-token science-heavy pre-training per arXiv 2508.15763) apache-2.0; internlm/Intern-S1-mini (8.5B) apache-2.0; UniParser/MolParser-7M cc-by-nc-sa-4.0; opendatalab/MinerU2.5-2509-1.2B agpl-3.0; deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-OCR (3.3B) mit; PaddlePaddle/PaddleOCR-VL apache-2.0; allenai/olmOCR-2-7B-1025 apache-2.0; google/gemma-3-4b-it gemma (gated). Chemistry-specialized VLMs did not deliver strong OCSR on real literature (F15: ChemVLM 4.79, ChemDFM-X 9.75, Intern-S1 18.98 SMILES exact match), so licensing plus quality favour fine-tuning a general Apache or MIT VLM.
  - sources: https://huggingface.co/OpenDFM/ChemDFM-X-v1.0-13B ; https://huggingface.co/AI4Chem/ChemVLM-26B-1-2 ; https://huggingface.co/internlm/Intern-S1 ; https://huggingface.co/internlm/Intern-S1-mini ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.15763 ; https://huggingface.co/datasets/UniParser/MolParser-7M ; https://huggingface.co/opendatalab/MinerU2.5-2509-1.2B ; https://huggingface.co/deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-OCR ; https://huggingface.co/google/gemma-3-4b-it ; https://huggingface.co/google/gemma-4-31B-it
- [OK] **F36** (lesson) OCSR evaluation needs careful normalization: public benchmarks contain label errors and tautomer mislabels, and protocols differ on R-group wildcards, abbreviation expansion, cis/trans and E-SMILES mapping. Exact match can therefore swing by many points depending on protocol.
  - details: COMO's protocol: canonicalize prediction and label with RDKit, replace R-groups with wildcards, keep tetrahedral chirality but ignore cis/trans (often missing from labels), use manually corrected CLEF/JPO/UOB from Xiong et al., and tautomer-standardize UOB. For WildMol-10K it expands abbreviated groups (e.g. Boc) back into the backbone and drops 111 samples for expansion or index conflicts. GTR-CoT shows SMILES canonicalization can misjudge correct graph predictions that contain superatoms, which motivated graph-level metrics (MolRec-Bench). MolRecBench-Wild introduces CARBON plus dual-track SMILES/CARBON scoring. MolSight uses an 'adaptive' metric that requires stereo correctness only when the label has stereo. olmOCR reports ±1.1 confidence intervals on a 1,400-PDF benchmark, a reminder that sub-1-point 'improvements' are noise.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.23546 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07553 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05832 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01982 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.19817 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2604.23546 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2506.07553 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2605.05832 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2607.01982 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2510.19817 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2608.03525
- [ADDED] MinerU.Chem (Aug 2026, Shanghai AI Lab) is a chemistry document-parsing system. It reports 93.02% SMILES exact match on the revised MolRecBench-Wild, against 74.87% for the best general model, GPT-5.6-Sol.
  - details: MinerU.Chem adds five modules to the MinerU pipeline: chemistry relevance filtering, MolYOLO molecule detection, molecule-identifier extraction, CARBON-graph OCSR and reaction-scheme parsing. Its outputs are a Molecule Summary List and a Reaction Summary List, exportable as MolFile/SMILES. Scores: 93.02% SMILES EM on the SMILES-evaluable subset (N=2,392) and 79.66% graph accuracy on all 5,024 images; Gemini-3.5-flash-thinking reaches 36.41% graph accuracy and Claude-opus-4-8 65.47% SMILES. Training data mixes curated open data, human-annotated real-literature crops and synthetic hard cases. This raises the realistic target well above the ~41% in F15, shows frontier closed VLMs are now usable teachers and baselines, and gives a ready template (detect → identifier → OCSR → summary tables) for the pinned evaluation.
  - sources: https://huggingface.co/papers/2608.03525 ; https://mineru.net/OpenSourceTools/Extractor
- [ADDED] MinerU2.5-Pro (Apr 2026) reached state of the art by changing only the data engine and training recipe on a fixed 1.2B architecture. It reports 95.69 on OmniDocBench v1.6, a new protocol.
  - details: The data grew from under 10M to 65.5M samples. Diversity- and difficulty-aware sampling was used, along with cross-model consistency verification for labels and difficulty, plus judge-and-refine render-then-verify correction of hard samples. Training ran in three stages: pre-training, hard-sample fine-tuning, then GRPO alignment. The paper fixes element-matching biases in OmniDocBench v1.5 and adds a Hard subset (v1.6). This maps directly onto the autoresearch idea of fixing the architecture and evaluation and iterating on data and recipe. It also means comparisons should now use v1.6, not v1.5.
  - sources: https://huggingface.co/papers/2604.04771
- [ADDED] MolSight (2026) requires an RDKit SVG as a side input, so its PubChem image-to-SMILES accuracy is not a pure OCSR result. A different, earlier OCSR model shares the name 'MolSight'.
  - details: The 2026 MolSight conditions on the image plus an RDKit-generated SVG holding atom symbols, bonds and coordinates. An SVG-only variant already reaches 0.46 accuracy, so the method does not apply to raster figures in literature or patents. The other MolSight (EfficientViT, MolScribe-like, pretrained on MolParser-7M) scores only 9.0 on WildMol-10K in COMO's table. Any pinned-benchmark comparison must check that inputs are image-only.
  - sources: https://huggingface.co/papers/2607.01982 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2604.23546
- [ADDED] Gemma 4 (Mar-May 2026, Apache-2.0) is a viable document-OCR base: it reports OmniDocBench 1.5 edit distances of 0.131 to 0.290 and offers variable per-image token budgets.
  - details: Model-card edit distances: 31B 0.131, 26B-A4B 0.149, 12B Unified 0.164, E4B 0.181, E2B 0.290, vs Gemma 3 27B 0.365. Visual token budgets are 70/140/280/560/1120, and high budgets are recommended for OCR. The 12B 'Unified' model is encoder-free, projecting raw patches linearly so the whole model fine-tunes in one pass. The 26B-A4B MoE has 3.8B active params. Liger-Kernel already patches Gemma4 multimodal.
  - sources: https://huggingface.co/google/gemma-4-31B-it ; https://huggingface.co/google/gemma-4-12B-it ; https://github.com/linkedin/Liger-Kernel
- [ADDED] Qwen3.6 (27B dense and 35B-A3B MoE, April 2026, Apache-2.0) is the newest Qwen VLM family. Like Qwen3.5, it is a hybrid Gated-DeltaNet model.
  - details: HF lists Qwen/Qwen3.6-27B and Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B (created 21 and 15 Apr 2026), both image-text-to-text and apache-2.0, on the qwen3_5 / qwen3_5_moe architectures; there are no small sizes. TRL warns that Qwen3.5/3.6 need flash-linear-attention, or the DeltaNet layers run about 20x slower. TRL also notes that their recurrent state leaks across padding-free packed rows. Packing is therefore a correctness risk, not just a speed option, for an automated train.py search.
  - sources: https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3.6-27B ; https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3.6-35B-A3B ; https://huggingface.co/docs/trl/async_grpo_trainer
- [ADDED] MolMole (LG AI Research, 2025) provides a page-level chemistry extraction pipeline and a 550-page benchmark with a page-level metric.
  - details: The pipeline unifies molecule detection, reaction-diagram parsing and OCSR on full pages. Its test set annotates bounding boxes, reaction labels and MOLfiles, with a new page-level metric, and the paper reports gains over DECIMER and OpenChemIE. It is useful for a page-level (not crop-level) pinned metric, so that detection and cropping errors are counted.
  - sources: https://huggingface.co/papers/2505.03777
- [ADDED] DianShi-RxnDB (Sep 2026) shows that fully automated patent-scale extraction works. It pulls about 24M reaction instances from USPTO and EPO patents (1976-2025), of which about 14.8M pass automated QC.
  - details: Built by the Shanghai AI Lab group behind GTR, MolRecBench-Wild and MinerU.Chem, the pipeline covers patent text, images and reaction schemes. Its automated qualification checks (61.7% pass) are an example of verifiable, label-free filters. These could act as cheap reward or validation signals, and the database is a possible source of weak or pseudo labels for patent-domain fine-tuning.
  - sources: https://huggingface.co/papers/2609.06703
- [ADDED] MarkushGrapher-2's code and its IP5-M benchmark are public under MIT, giving a concrete Markush metric: CXSMILES accuracy of 53.7 on IP5-M and 48.0 on WildMol-M.
  - details: The GitHub README lists the CVPR 2026 paper, the IP5-M benchmark (1,000 manually annotated Markush structures from patents) and the results table. This makes IP5-M a ready, pinned, patent-specific Markush validation set with CXSMILES accuracy as its scalar. That suits a prepare.py-style fixed evaluation better than SMILES exact match, which cannot express Markush.
  - sources: https://github.com/DS4SD/MarkushGrapher ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.28550
- [ADDED] Doc2SAR's DocSAR-200 cannot be downloaded as-is. Only DOIs and ground truth are released, so the PDFs must be re-acquired before using it as a pinned end-to-end SAR metric.
  - details: The repo ships end2end.py, eval.py, data/ground_truth and doi.txt, and states that PDFs are excluded for size reasons. Its Table Recall metric is strict: a row counts only if the SMILES and all linked activity values are correct. That fits the goal's 'structure + property' joint target, but reproducibility depends on publisher access.
  - sources: https://github.com/Jesse-zjx/Doc2SAR
- [ADDED] Evidence on fully autonomous research agents (Aug 2026) shows they fail mainly through a missing metacognitive loop. This is relevant to running a NEVER-STOP autoresearch loop on a noisy OCSR metric.
  - details: AutoResearchEval ran 100 frontier-science tasks across 8 harness-model combinations, yielding 800 trajectories and a 45-pattern failure taxonomy. Failures converge on agents not checking outputs against evidence or revising their path. For this project, that argues for guardrails in program.md: re-evaluate any 'keep' on a held-out split, use CI or multi-seed thresholds (olmOCR-Bench ±1.1), and audit protocol changes. The VLM loop's metric is far noisier than val_bpb.
  - sources: https://huggingface.co/papers/2608.14905

### Implications (researcher's recommendations)
- Inner-loop base model: use Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct or 4B-Instruct (Apache-2.0, OCRBench 858 and 881, 32px per token, supported by LLaMA-Factory, ms-swift, TRL, Unsloth and EasyR1) in a fixed prepare.py. Promote the winning train.py recipes to Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct or Qwen3.5-4B/9B in a slower outer tier. Use Instruct or non-thinking modes only (Thinking variants score lower on OCR; Qwen3.5 thinks by default). For Qwen3.5, install flash-linear-attention and avoid naive packing (DeltaNet state carries across packed rows). Skip Gemma 3 (fixed 896px / 256 tokens, OCRBench 702 at 12B).
- Budget: VLM experiments carry about 1-2 minutes of fixed overhead (model load, image decode, vLLM evaluation), so a 5-minute budget is too short. Use a fixed 15-20 minute wall-clock training budget, excluding load and compile, for the crop-level tier (2B LoRA or full fine-tuning: about 10k-50k molecule crops per run) and 30-45 minutes for a page-level tier. Calibrate with a 60-second throughput probe on the real hardware and record samples/s, tokens/s and vision tokens per sample in results.tsv.
- Single scalar val metric in fixed prepare.py: a weighted 'val_score' (higher is better) over a pinned, de-duplicated validation set. Suggested components: (a) OCSR exact match after RDKit canonicalization, stereo-aware with the adaptive rule, on real literature and patent crops (MolRecBench-Wild-style plus corrected CLEF/JPO/UOB plus your own drug-discovery papers); (b) mean Tanimoto as a tie-breaker; (c) Markush exact CXSMILES/E-SMILES plus R-table accuracy; (d) strict row-level SAR table recall (Doc2SAR-style: SMILES and every activity value correct); (e) field-level F1 for properties with unit and qualifier normalization. Put all canonicalization, normalization and reward code in prepare.py so the agent cannot game the metric.
- Keep-or-discard noise threshold: run the baseline 3 times with different seeds and keep a change only if val_score beats the running best by at least 2 standard deviations (olmOCR's own CI is ±1.1 points). Otherwise the greedy git-reset loop will chase noise. A final optional step can average checkpoints from the top-k kept runs ('souping', as in olmOCR 2).
- Forgetting guard as a hard constraint: add a small fixed general-capability probe (e.g. 200 DocVQA/OCRBench/ChartQA items) to prepare.py and auto-discard any run that drops it by more than about 2 points, like autoresearch's VRAM soft constraint. In program.md, tell the agent to prefer tuning attention projections or MLP gate/up, keep the ViT frozen (or unfreeze only the top blocks at about 10x lower LR), and replay 10-30% general document or OCR data; these are hypotheses for the agent to test.
- Fair search space in train.py: LoRA rank and alpha, target modules (attention projections only / gate+up / all-linear / plus merger), trainable ViT blocks, separate LRs for ViT, merger and LLM (search ranges: LoRA 5e-5 to 3e-4; selective or full fine-tuning 5e-6 to 2e-5), min/max_pixels (the main cost lever), data mixture ratios (synthetic RDKit/Indigo/CDK renders vs real patent and paper crops vs general replay), augmentations (scan noise, wavy and wedge bonds, foreign-language text, abbreviation superatoms), label smoothing, answer-only loss, packing on or off, and Liger fused cross-entropy. Keep renderers, tokenization caches and the data pool fixed in prepare.py.
- Output representation is a first-class search dimension but must be scored the same way. Let train.py choose between plain canonical SMILES, a graph-traversal chain of thought followed by SMILES (GTR-style), atom-and-bond JSON, and E-SMILES/CXSMILES for Markush; prepare.py always converts the final answer to a canonical molecule. Keep abbreviations as superatoms in both labels and outputs ('faithfully recognize what you've seen').
- Structured property output: use a flat, fixed-key record per measurement (compound_id, smiles_or_ref, target, assay, endpoint (IC50/Ki/Kd/EC50/%inh/...), qualifier (=,<,>,~), value, unit, conditions, source_location). Prefer YAML or JSON-lines over deeply nested JSON (olmOCR saw fewer retries and repetition loops with YAML). At evaluation, use schema-constrained decoding (XGrammar or Guidance in vLLM) with a max_tokens cap and an n-gram repetition guard. Record parse failures and timeouts as 'crash' rows rather than as zero-score keeps.
- Add RL only after the SFT recipe plateaus, and as a switchable phase inside the same fixed budget. Start with COMO-style interleaved MLE plus a sequence-level reward (N=8-32 samples, temperature 0.3-0.5, reward ≈ 0.1·valid + 0.5·similarity + 0.4·exact, loss weight about 0.1) or a short GRPO/DAPO/GSPO run with LoRA and vLLM colocate on 2B-4B models. Use KL about 0.01 or an MLE anchor and give invalid JSON or SMILES reward 0. Explicitly watch exact match (MolSeek-OCR's GSPO raised graph match but lowered exact SMILES) and an out-of-distribution scan subset (Re3-DAPO's BIVP variant collapsed into repetition).
- Keep reward functions CPU-side and deterministic (RDKit validity, canonical exact match, Tanimoto, normalized edit distance, order-invariant set-F1 for table rows and reactions, unit-normalized numeric match with tolerance). These double as validation metrics, which keeps SFT and RL experiments comparable.
- Two-stage document pipeline as a fixed baseline: a page parser (PaddleOCR-VL-0.9B, Apache-2.0, about 1.3 pages/s on A100) for layout, crops and tables, plus the fine-tuned VLM for structure and SAR linking. Let the agent decide whether end-to-end page extraction beats the pipeline on the same val_score; do not hard-code the answer.
- Data and licensing: avoid MolParser-7M (CC-BY-NC-SA) and AGPL models (ChemDFM-X, MinerU2.5) if the result must be commercial. Synthesize unlimited labelled crops by rendering PubChem/ChEMBL SMILES in several styles, generate Markush and SAR-table pages programmatically from known structures, and reserve human-verified real crops from target-domain papers and patents for validation only.
- Extend results.tsv beyond autoresearch: commit, val_score, component metrics (ocsr_em, tanimoto, markush_em, sar_row_recall, prop_f1), general_probe, samples_seen, tokens_per_s, mean_vision_tokens, peak_vram_gb, status, description. Have analysis.ipynb plot the frontier against samples_seen as well as time, so you can tell data-efficiency wins from throughput wins.
- In program.md, also give the agent compute-sanity rules: do not raise max_pixels and the model size in the same experiment; prefer deleting code or modules when the score is equal (the simplicity criterion); never edit prepare.py, evaluation or rewards; time out at 1.5x the budget; and treat a lost forgetting guard or a crashed run as an automatic git reset.

### Open questions
- Measured VLM SFT throughput on the actual target hardware (H100/H200) for Qwen3-VL-2B/4B/8B and Qwen3.5 with packing, FlashAttention-2 and Liger was not found in primary sources. The estimates in F33/F34 need a 60-second calibration run.
- Exact GTR-VL v3 RL gains (format, graph and SMILES rewards on hand-drawn data), MarkushGrapher-2's exact numbers and MolParser's Markush/throughput figures were not verified in primary text. The web-search budget ran out during this session; a follow-up session could finish verification.
- Can a fine-tuned 2B-8B general VLM beat the best specialist OCSR models (COMO, MolScribe/MolNexTR, GTR-VL) on real 2025-2026 literature such as MolRecBench-Wild, or is a hybrid (specialist OCSR plus VLM for layout, SAR linking and properties) the better production design?
- How much RL helps within a 20-40 minute single-node budget for short structured outputs (SMILES or JSON rows) compared with spending the same compute on more SFT data. Published RL gains used multi-hour to 70-hour runs.
- Best Markush representation for both training and scoring (CXSMILES, E-SMILES, core plus R-group table, or CARBON) and how to make val_score robust to equivalent representations.
- No public, permissively licensed benchmark exists for property/activity extraction (IC50/Ki/ADMET/PK with units, qualifiers and assay context) from drug-discovery papers and patents. DocSAR-200's release status and license need checking, and an in-house pinned val set is probably required.
- Whether Qwen3.5's natively multimodal hybrid (Gated DeltaNet) architecture fine-tunes as stably as Qwen3-VL for dense OCR, and whether frameworks handle its packing correctly (TRL warns about recurrent state across packed rows).
- How much general-data replay is needed to hold general OCR/DocVQA ability when training heavily on synthetic molecule renders. The forgetting literature tested other target skills (counting, medical VQA, OCR), not chemistry.

## Topic: data-sources

### Summary
Good paired training and evaluation data exists for structure recognition (images to SMILES, molfiles or Markush). Data for recognizing properties and activity in context, tied to a page or table, is scarce, licensed in mixed ways, and mostly has to be built. For structures, the strongest open real-image source is the USPTO bulk full text with embedded images. Since 2001, grants and applications have shipped "complex work units": chemistry drawings as TIF images plus the ChemDraw CDX and MOL files behind them. MolScribe (680K pairs), USPTO-30K, MarkushGrapher-2's USPTO-MOL-M (54,785 real Markush pairs) and COMO were all built this way. The labels are noisy: the stored connection table or wildcard atoms often do not match the drawing, so they need remapping or correction. The large open crop corpora are MolParser-7M (7.72M synthetic plus 91K human-labelled real crops, CC BY-NC-SA, non-commercial), MolGallery (10.3M real crops pseudo-labelled where 3 models agree, gated), MolGrapher-300K and PatCID (81M patent images and about 14M unique structures, extracted by IBM). The Markush sets from IBM's MarkushGrapher 1 and 2 (M2S, USPTO-Markush, IP5-M) are CC BY 4.0. Evidence from 2025-2026 consistently shows that synthetic-only training transfers poorly to real documents. In MolParser, augmentation plus a curriculum raised WildMol-10K accuracy from 40.1% to 76.9%. Adding just 9.5% real images more than doubled a Qwen2.5-VL model's ACS exact match (0.15 to 0.37). New real-literature benchmarks (MolRecBench-Wild, BioVista) show frontier VLMs and older specialist models far below their patent-benchmark scores. For activity ground truth, ChEMBL (CC BY-SA 3.0), BindingDB (CC BY 3.0/4.0, about 1.32M patent-derived measurements from 8,587 US patents by Feb 2026), Papyrus, ExCAPE-DB and PubChem are document-linked but not page-linked. ChEMBL provides DOI/patent IDs and compound_key, the compound's label in the source document, but no table coordinates. Page-level supervision has to be reconstructed by aligning values and compound labels against parsed open-access PDFs or USPTO XML tables. 2025-2026 extraction benchmarks show how hard this is: DocSAR-200 (best table recall 80.78%), BioVista (end-to-end triplet F1 about 0.32) and HARVEST/H-Bench (3.15M LLM-extracted patent records). On licensing, patents are the safest commercial source; US patent text is generally public domain, but some drawings may carry 37 CFR 1.71 copyright notices. Papers are usable only through licensed open-access subsets (PMC commercial-use subset, CC BY on ChemRxiv/bioRxiv) or negotiated text-and-data-mining (TDM) or AI licences. Elsevier's TDM terms are non-commercial and Wiley requires a licence for AI training. Case law is still unsettled (Bartz v. Anthropic, Thomson Reuters v. Ross, LAION, GEMA v OpenAI). I could not open several primary pages (EBI, Nature, Zenodo, arXiv HTML) directly and the web-search budget ran out, so some numbers come from search summaries; confidence is marked accordingly.

### Findings
- [UNVERIFIED] **F1** (existence) USPTO bulk full-text-with-images products have included chemical-structure complex work units (CDX + MOL + TIF) for grants and applications since 2001, and now live on the USPTO Open Data Portal (launched Feb 12, 2025).
  - details: Catalog descriptions say the grant full-text-with-embedded-images dataset (product PTGRDT, from Jan 2, 2001) and the application publication dataset (from Mar 15, 2001) contain full text, drawings, and complex work units: tables, math, chemical structures, and sequences. Chemical structures are external CambridgeSoft ChemDraw (CDX) files and MDL MOL files, plus CCITT G4 TIFF images. The text-only grant product (1976 onward) references the CDX/MOL files but does not include them. Grants are released weekly on Tuesdays; each weekly application file holds about 5,000 publications. The ODP (data.uspto.gov) replaces the old Bulk Data Storage System; Google's Red Book mirror stopped updating in 2015. The first years (2001-2004) use different SGML/XML DTDs (noted by Subramanian et al. 2023). I could not open data.uspto.gov to confirm the current file layout.
  - checker note: uspto.gov, data.uspto.gov, catalog.data.gov and google.com were all blocked by the session's egress policy. From general knowledge, these points are consistent: application publications start in March 2001, and grant and application full-text-with-images products carry CDX, MOL and TIF chemical complex work units. The ODP launch date (Feb 12, 2025), the weekly file sizes and the DTD notes could not be checked.
  - sources: https://www.uspto.gov/subscription-center/2025/uspto-launches-new-open-data-portal-easy-quick-access-data ; https://catalog.data.gov/dataset/patent-grant-full-text-1976-present ; https://data.amerigeoss.org/dataset/patent-application-publication-full-text-2001-present ; https://www.google.com/googlebooks/uspto-patents-redbook.html ; https://pubs.rsc.org/en/content/articlehtml/2023/dd/d3dd00041a
- [OK] **F2** (lesson) USPTO ChemDraw/MOL attachments are large in volume but noisy as labels: the stored connection table often differs from what the image shows.
  - details: NextMove counted more than 24M ChemDraw files in US patents from 2001 onward (2016 talk); a 2023 poster says 44M ChemDraw files have shipped with USPTO publications. NextMove's analysis: naively exporting the connection table in a sketch can give artificial or wrong structures. Examples include tables drawn with ChemDraw tools that export as cyclobutane grids and author valence errors kept as drawn. Their Praline tool corrects and categorises sketches. COMO (2026) had to remap wildcard '*' atoms in USPTO MOL files to the labels visible in the image (R, X, Me), keeping 652K of the 680K MolScribe USPTO pairs. MolScribe also calls its USPTO data noisy: atom coordinates come from the MOL files, not the image. I did not obtain an exact image-vs-MOL mismatch rate.
  - sources: https://nextmovesoftware.com/talks/Lowe_SketchySketches_ACS_201608.pdf ; https://nextmovesoftware.com/posters/Mayfield_SketchySketchesII_Sheffield_202306.pdf ; https://nextmovesoftware.com/blog/?p=1852 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.23546 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2205.14311 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2604.23546 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2205.14311 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2506.07553
- [FIXED] **F3** (existence) MolScribe's training data is 1M PubChem molecules rendered with Indigo during training plus 680K USPTO image/MOL pairs (1.68M total), downloadable from Hugging Face with no data license stated.
  - details: MolScribe (Qian et al., JCIM 2023) leaves out patents that overlap its benchmarks. HF repo yujieq/MolScribe holds uspto_mol.zip (2.18 GB), pubchem.zip (90 MB), real.zip (benchmarks), synthetic.zip and perturb.zip, plus checkpoints such as swin_base_char_aux_1m680k.pth. The GitHub code is MIT; no license is stated for the data or checkpoints. Figure 6 of the paper: training on both synthetic and patent data beats either alone. Patent-only training does well on patent benchmarks (CLEF, USPTO, Staker) but poorly elsewhere. MarkushGrapher-2 reuses 243K of these real pairs in its phase-1 training.
  - CORRECTION: The training data is 1M PubChem molecules rendered with Indigo plus 680K USPTO pairs (1.68M), and benchmark patents are excluded; the file sizes are correct (uspto_mol.zip 2.18 GB, pubchem.zip 89.8 MB, three checkpoints including swin_base_char_aux_1m680k.pth). However, the HF repo yujieq/MolScribe that holds the data and checkpoints is tagged license: mit in its README metadata. GitHub states MIT for the code only. So the claim that no license is stated is wrong: the HF repo declares MIT. Whether MIT can actually cover USPTO-derived images is a separate question.
  - checker note: The claim in Figure 6 that combined synthetic and patent data beats either alone is confirmed in the text. The per-benchmark claim that patent-only training is poor elsewhere could not be read from the figure. The MarkushGrapher-2 use of 243K MolScribe real pairs is confirmed on its HF card.

- [FIXED] **F4** (license) MolParser-7M on HF is the largest open OCSR training set, but it is CC BY-NC-SA 4.0 (non-commercial; commercial use by contacting DP Technology).
  - details: HF UniParser/MolParser-7M configs: pretrain_synthetic_7M (7,720,468 image/E-SMILES pairs, about 122 GB); sft_real (91,166 human-labelled real crops); valid (403); test_simple_10k (WildMol-10K); test_markush_10k (WildMol-10K-M). Labels use E-SMILES: RDKit SMILES, then <sep>, then XML-like tags for R-groups/abbreviations (<a>), uncertain ring attachments (<r>), abstract rings (<c>) and connection points (<dum>). About 400K real samples were annotated in total; part of the SFT set is held back for a competition. Synthetic molecules come from ChEMBL, PubChem and Kaggle BMS plus random Markush, rendered with RDKit and Indigo. ICCV 2025.
  - CORRECTION: MolParser-7M is CC BY-NC-SA 4.0 (non-commercial; commercial use by contacting DP Technology). Its configs are pretrain_synthetic_7M (7,720,468 pairs, about 122 GB download), sft_real (91,166), valid (403), test_simple_10k and test_markush_10k, and part of the SFT set is held back for a competition. It is no longer the largest open OCSR training set: MolGallery (UniParser, Sept 2026) holds about 10.33M real-document image/E-SMILES pairs, is gated on HF, and is also CC BY-NC-SA 4.0.
  - checker note: These details are correct: the E-SMILES tags, the sources (ChEMBL, PubChem, BMS plus random Markush, rendered with RDKit and Indigo), about 400K real annotations and ICCV 2025.
  - sources: https://huggingface.co/datasets/UniParser/MolParser-7M ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.11098 ; https://huggingface.co/datasets/UniParser/MolGallery ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2411.11098 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2609.05807
- [OK] **F5** (method) MolParser's data engine is a working recipe for cheap real labels: detect, deduplicate, ensemble-disagreement sampling, pre-annotation, double review, retrain every 80K labels.
  - details: A YOLO11 detector (MolDet) ran on 1.22M real PDFs: patents from several offices plus open-access bioRxiv, medRxiv and ChemRxiv papers. That gave more than 20M molecule crops, cut to 4M by p-hash deduplication. Five models trained with 5-fold splits predict each crop; mean pairwise Tanimoto gives a confidence. Crops are sampled at random from the 0.6-0.9 confidence band (hard but learnable). Annotators correct model pre-annotations, which cuts time from over 3 min to about 30 s per molecule (about 90% saving). Two annotators review each one. After every 80K new labels the models are retrained, reaching 400K in total. 56.04% of final labels were accepted as pre-annotated; 20.97% needed one correction, 13.87% two rounds, 9.13% three or more. Some synthetic data is kept during fine-tuning. Results: WildMol-10K accuracy 76.9% (MolParser-Base) vs MolScribe 66.4% and MolGrapher 45.5%.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.11098 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2411.11098
- [FIXED] **F6** (number) Augmentation and curriculum matter as much as data volume: in ablations, real-style augmentation roughly doubled accuracy on in-the-wild crops and on a rendering engine not seen in training.
  - details: MolParser (Table 5, WildMol-10K accuracy): no augmentation and no curriculum 40.1%; augmentation 69.5%; augmentation plus curriculum 76.9%. The curriculum starts with no augmentation and molecules under 60 SMILES tokens, then raises augmentation strength and length. MolScribe (Table 3, 200K Indigo-rendered images): accuracy on ChemDraw renders (not seen in training) is 89.7% with augmentation vs 72.1% without; baseline image-to-SMILES model 82.5% vs 57.2%. In-domain Indigo accuracy changes much less (95.9 vs 91.4). MolScribe's augmentation: swap functional groups for abbreviations (SMARTS list with substitution probabilities); add R-group superatoms; vary Indigo font, bond width and length; rotation, padding, cropping and Gaussian noise.
  - CORRECTION: In MolParser Table 5 (WildMol-10K), accuracy goes from 40.1 with no augmentation to 69.5 with augmentation (+29.4 points, about 1.7x) and to 76.9 with curriculum added. In MolScribe Table 3 (200K Indigo images), ChemDraw out-of-domain accuracy is 89.7 vs 72.1 (+17.6 points, about 1.24x), the image-to-SMILES baseline is 82.5 vs 57.2 (+25.3 points, about 1.44x), and in-domain Indigo is 95.9 vs 91.4. Augmentation gives large out-of-domain gains, but it does not 'roughly double' accuracy, and MolParser does not call its augmentation 'real-style'.
  - checker note: The curriculum description (no augmentation and fewer than 60 SMILES tokens first, then stronger augmentation) and the MolScribe augmentation list (SMARTS abbreviation substitution, R-group superatoms, random-character superatoms, Indigo style variation, rotation, padding, cropping, noise) are confirmed.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.11098 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2205.14311 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2411.11098 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2205.14311
- [UNVERIFIED] **F7** (number) A 2026 controlled study shows real labelled images close most of the synthetic-to-real gap even at small fractions, and the best base VLM depends on the real-data mix.
  - details: 'Real Data Closes Synthetic-to-Real Gap in OCSR' (Guan et al., arXiv 2608.09100, Aug 2026) fine-tuned 21 recognizers on synthetic/real mixes. Qwen2.5-VL ACS exact match: 0.15 with 0% real, 0.37 with 9.5% real, 0.46 with 50.2% real; the trend repeats across three base models. LoRA on the vision tower did nothing for Qwen but added 22.8 to 34.6 points for InternVL3-8B. Best configuration: 0.96 exact match on clean renders; 0.49 ACS, 0.65 CLEF-IP, 0.84 UOB, 0.76 USPTO. Gaps between base models shrink as real data is added. Figures come from the abstract and search snippets; I did not read the full PDF.
  - checker note: arXiv was blocked, and 2608.09100 is not indexed on Hugging Face Papers, which did index the other 2026 OCSR papers (COMO, MolSeek, MolRecBench-Wild, MinerU.Chem, MolParser-Mobile). The analyst also says the numbers come only from the abstract and search snippets. Existence, the authors and every figure (0.15/0.37/0.46, LoRA +22.8 to +34.6 points, 0.96/0.49/0.65/0.84/0.76) are unconfirmed and should not be relied on.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09100
- [UNVERIFIED] **F8** (lesson) Agreement between several recognizers is a strong filter for pseudo-labels; checking a prediction by re-rendering it and comparing images is close to chance.
  - details: VERDICT (arXiv 2608.22183, Aug 2026), on 263 ACS journal depictions with definitive ground truth: re-render image similarity AUROC 0.547 [0.465, 0.629]; agreement of four architecturally different recognizers AUROC 0.916 [0.880, 0.952]. The ordering repeats on CLEF-IP, UOB and USPTO. The agreement gate gives 0.888 precision at 81.7% coverage and 0.985 at 52.1%, at about 3.5 s per image. MolGallery (DP Technology) applies the same idea at scale: it keeps only real-document crops where two MolParser-1.0 variants and MolScribe produce identical E-SMILES, yielding 10.3M pairs (193 GB). Note: an auto-generated outside review says the pixel-space negative result is over-sold.
  - checker note: The MolGallery part is confirmed: three-model consensus (two MolParser-1.0 variants plus MolScribe) with identical E-SMILES required, about 10M images, 193 GB, 10,327,534 rows on HF. VERDICT (arXiv 2608.22183) could not be found: arXiv was blocked and the paper is not on HF Papers. All AUROC, precision and coverage numbers are unverified, so the headline 'lesson' rests on an unconfirmed source.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22183 ; https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05807 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2609.05807 ; https://huggingface.co/datasets/UniParser/MolGallery
- [OK] **F9** (method) MolParser-Mobile / MolParser 1.5 (2025-2026) train on more than 18M pairs: 7.72M synthetic plus 10.3M pseudo-labelled real, then 91.2K human-labelled SFT, on-policy distillation, and DPO on 500 preference pairs.
  - details: MolParser-Mobile has 9.98M parameters and runs 1,520 molecules/s on an RTX 4090D (78 molecules/s on a 14-vCPU Xeon). Training: pretrain on MolParser-7M plus MolGallery, then SFT on MolParser-SFT (91.2K) with a 200K replay buffer, then on-policy distillation from MolParser 1.5, then DPO on 500 (image, correct, plausible-wrong) triples targeting Markush and chirality errors. BioVista OCSR overall accuracy: MolParser-Mobile 0.801, MolParser 1.0 0.703, MolMiner 0.507, MolScribe 0.455, MolNexTR 0.401, DECIMER 0.298. On the Uni-Parser benchmark, Gemma4-31B scores 0.073 and Qwen3.5-397B 0.272. MolGallery is gated on HF; MolDet-Bench is CC BY-NC-SA 4.0.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05807 ; https://huggingface.co/datasets/UniParser/MolGallery ; https://huggingface.co/datasets/UniParser/MolDet-Bench ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2609.05807 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2512.15098
- [OK] **F10** (lesson) Uni-Parser (DP Technology, Dec 2025) found that generic synthetic layout pretraining hurts once large real data exists. Synthetic data helps only when derived from real layouts.
  - details: Layout model trained on 500K pages, 220K of them human-annotated with group labels (molecule paired with its identifier and Markush description; table with caption). In-house corpora: 170M pages of scientific literature, 140M patent pages and 20M FinePDFs pages. Pretraining on DocSynth300K was 'counterproductive' because of low-fidelity rendering and style mismatch. What worked: synthetic pages made from real layouts by merging elements, perturbing positions and substituting content. Table structure model (SLANet) trained on 1M tables, including in-house synthetic line-based tables with molecule, chart and formula cells. The data engine (synthetic bootstrap, then active learning, then self-training with ensemble pseudo-labels) cut annotation volume by 95%. Graph-based (atom-bond) OCSR was dropped: it needed more than 20x the annotation effort of end-to-end models and had a lower ceiling.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.15098 ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2512.15098
- [OK] **F11** (number) Detecting molecules on whole pages can be trained mostly on real human-annotated pages with a small synthetic share. Detection mAP50 is about 0.99 on the open MolDet-Bench-Doc benchmark.
  - details: MolDetv2-Doc-n (YOLO11n, 960x960) was trained on more than 60K human-annotated PDF pages (patents, papers, books) plus 10K synthetic PDF pages. Hard negatives (equations, ball-and-stick renderings, symbols) cut false positives. On MolDet-Bench-Doc (447 pages, 2,178 molecules, from 50 patents across 20 offices and 100 open-access articles): mAP50 0.9936, mAP50:95 0.9544, 3.1 ms on a T4. MolDet-Bench-General has 799 images covering single molecules, reactions, tables, handwriting and pages. The benchmark is CC BY-NC-SA 4.0.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05807 ; https://huggingface.co/datasets/UniParser/MolDet-Bench ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2609.05807
- [FIXED] **F12** (existence) MolGrapher-Synthetic-300K and USPTO-30K (IBM) supply atom-keypoint supervision and a 3-way split of patent molecules (clean / abbreviated / over 70 atoms).

PubChem SMILES rendered with RDKit, with 12 molecule-level augmentations (explicit H/methyl/carbons, superatoms with one or more attachment points, wedge/dash, wavy bonds, and others) and 12 rendering-level ones (bond width, font, font size, padding, rotation, atom indices, hand-drawn style, charge styles, aromatic circles, and others). Includes MOL and keypoints. The card states no license. PatCID's IBM pipeline uses MolGrapher.
  - CORRECTION: MolGrapher-Synthetic-300K has 219,068/27,383/27,384 splits, PubChem SMILES rendered with RDKit, 12 molecule-level and 12 rendering-level augmentations, MOL plus keypoints, and no license on the card. USPTO-30K's paper and card say each molecule was 'independently selected among all the available images from 2001 to 2020'. This is independent sampling across all images, not a guarantee that each molecule comes from a different document. The subsets are clean, with superatoms, and clean with more than 70 atoms; the card also states no license.
  - checker note: This is a minor overstatement of how USPTO-30K was sampled.
  - sources: https://huggingface.co/datasets/docling-project/MolGrapher-Synthetic-300K ; https://huggingface.co/datasets/docling-project/USPTO-30K ; https://github.com/DS4SD/PatCID ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2308.12234
- [FIXED] **F13** (method) MarkushGrapher (CVPR 2025) shows Markush data can be synthesized: CXSMILES augmentation, CDK rendering, template text plus LLM paraphrase. It also releases small real CC BY 4.0 benchmarks.
  - details: Pipeline: sample diverse PubChem SMILES; augment to CXSMILES with RDKit (R-groups, 'm' position-variation and 'Sg' frequency-variation sections); render with CDK at random drawing settings; get OCR cells by parsing CDK SVG aligned to MOL; generate substituent text from templates and lists taken from real patents; paraphrase 10% with Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3, giving the substituent table as context so meaning is unchanged. Trained on 210K synthetic images, about 10 epochs on one H100. Benchmarks: M2S (103 real crops with text, USPTO/EPO/WIPO), USPTO-Markush (74-75), MarkushGrapher-Synthetic (1,000). CXSMILES exact match on M2S: MarkushGrapher 38%, MolScribe 21%, DECIMER 3%, GPT-4o 0%. On USPTO-Markush: 32% vs 7%. HF docling-project/MarkushGrapher-Datasets, cc-by-4.0.
  - CORRECTION: The pipeline details are correct: PubChem SMILES chosen for substructure diversity, RDKit CXSMILES augmentation, CDK rendering, OCR cells from parsing CDK SVG aligned to MOL, templates mined from patents, and 10% paraphrased with Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3. Training used 210K synthetic images for 10 epochs on one H100, and the datasets are CC BY 4.0. However, GPT-4o's CXSMILES exact match on M2S is 4%, not 0% (it is 0% on MarkushGrapher-Synthetic and 0% on full Markush exact match). M2S CXSMILES exact match for the others: MarkushGrapher 38, MolScribe 21, DECIMER 3. USPTO-Markush: 32 vs 7, with 74 images in the table and 75 in the text.
  - checker note: The full Markush exact match (CXSMILES and substituent table both correct) on M2S is only 10% for MarkushGrapher.
  - sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16096 ; https://huggingface.co/datasets/docling-project/MarkushGrapher-Datasets ; https://huggingface.co/papers/2503.16096

  - details: HF docling-project/MarkushGrapher-2-Datasets: uspto-mol-m-54k (54,785 train / 200 test; real USPTO images, CXSMILES generated from the MOL files, OCR cells predicted by ChemicalOCR); uspto-markush (74); m2s (103); ip5-markush (878 test). Each sample has page_image (about 1024x1024), OCR cells with bounding boxes, cxsmiles, and cxsmiles_opt (a tokenizer-friendly form).
